## Lesson 1: What this module refuses to tell you

Stable ID: m20-l01-v1. Core.

Career advice is the area where confident claims are cheapest to make and most expensive to believe.

Bring: A published portfolio.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A written statement of what this course does not claim about hiring
- An evidence plan naming sources, fields and a date window
- A rule excluding aggregator counts as evidence
- A statement of what your planned sample cannot support

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: What the course’s two captures establish, and what they do not.

**Words you will use**

- **Two dated captures:** Two vacancies, retrieved on two dates. Evidence that those employers published those words on those days, and nothing wider.
- **Not a study:** A sample of two, not designed, not representative of anything. The course says so rather than rounding it up into guidance.
- **A window:** A fixed period, about two weeks, in which everything is gathered. It makes the sample datable and stops it drifting across months.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Writing an evidence plan, and writing it after starting to gather. Opened career pages and started copying anything that looked relevant into a document, meaning to tidy it up once I saw what was there.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “What the course’s two vacancy captures do establish”.

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example plan: at least twelve vacancies from employers' own career pages, gathered within one two-week window. Each is recorded with employer, URL, retrieval date, exact title and stated seniority, plus three fit fields kept apart: who the employer says it can hire and from where; where the work happens (remote, or the city and office days for hybrid and on-site roles); and any required hours or overlap, converted to IST. Aggregators may be used to find employer names and are never cited as evidence. The plan states in advance what twelve vacancies cannot show — demand, pay, how common a requirement is across the market, or whether any of them would consider the learner — and that the sample expires and must be re-gathered if the search continues past a month.

### Keep this practice material beside you

Section: learn. Stable action: supplied-material.

Use your own material, or the labelled practice material below. Keep its source labels attached; practice material is never evidence about real people.

- The course’s own record, not made up: RESOURCE-LIBRARY.md row V01 and its vacancy table, gathered on 6 September 2026 from employers’ own boards, without an account.
- Eight vacancy URLs were attempted. Captured: one full description (Atomicwork, Lead Product Designer, Bengaluru, hybrid, India-based, minimum three years) and one partial (HackerRank, Senior Product Designer, Bangalore: the application form loaded, the description did not, and the form asked about relocating and attending an office three days a week). Neither showed a posting date.
- Not captured: six listings had already expired to the employer’s board index, and one host returned HTTP 403, a refusal.
- The record lists nine outcomes for eight URLs and does not say which URL had two. Treat both captures as expired until re-checked; together they cannot show demand, pay, how common any requirement is, or what a junior role expects.

### Write the evidence plan

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

An evidence plan naming a count, sources, a window and the fields for each listing.

- Decide how many vacancies, from where, and over what window.
- List the fields you will record for each, keeping eligibility, work location and hours as three separate fields.

**Start here:** Write the plan as a short list before opening a single listing.

**Enough:** Somebody else could gather your sample from the plan alone.

**A window:** A fixed period, about two weeks, in which everything is gathered. It makes the sample datable and stops it drifting across months.

**Fields decided first:** What you record for every listing. Deciding after you start means the early captures are missing what the later ones have.

**Three fit fields:** Who the employer can hire and from where; where the work happens; what hours it needs, in IST. Remote answers only the second, so each gets its own field.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Writing an evidence plan, and writing it after starting to gather.

**What I did:** Opened career pages and started copying anything that looked relevant into a document, meaning to tidy it up once I saw what was there.

**What the first six captures held:** Employer, title and requirements. No URL, no retrieval date, and no note of what the listing said about location.

**What I noticed at the seventh:** That eligibility was the thing I actually wanted to know, and I had not recorded it for any of the first six.

**What that cost:** Three of the six listings had already gone from the employer’s page. The information exists nowhere now and cannot be recovered.

**What the plan fixed:** Eight named fields, written down before the next capture, and every listing after that one is complete and comparable.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is gathering first and deciding the fields once you have seen a few, because you do not yet know what matters. Vacancies vanish within weeks, so an incomplete early capture is permanently incomplete.

**Trade-off:** Deciding eight fields in advance means recording things that turn out not to matter, and that cost is a minute per listing.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether eight fields are the right eight. Two of mine were never used, and the missing ninth would have been salary, which almost nothing states.

### Set the source rule

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

A rule saying that only employers’ own pages count as evidence, and how an aggregator may still be used.

- Write that only employers' own pages count as evidence.
- Write how aggregators may be used without being cited.

**Start here:** Write the rule as one sentence you can check a capture against.

**Enough:** The rule distinguishes finding a name from citing a number.

**The employer’s own page:** Where the employer publishes what they wrote. An aggregator’s copy may be edited, stale, or reposted by somebody else entirely.

**Finding versus citing:** An aggregator is a good index and a bad source. Use it to learn that a company exists and is hiring, then go to the company.

### State the limits in advance

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

What the sample cannot show, written before gathering, with an expiry date.

- Write what your sample will not be able to show.
- Include that it expires and when you would re-gather.

**Start here:** Write the limits now, while you have no findings to protect.

**Enough:** At least one limit would weaken a finding you would like to have.

**Limits written first:** Written before the data exists, so they are not shaped by what you found. This is the same discipline Module 15 used for measurement.

**Expiry:** The date after which the sample describes a hiring market that has moved on. Vacancies are among the shortest-lived evidence there is.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-demo.

Made-up example. Writing the limits of a vacancy sample, and writing them afterwards.

**What I did:** Gathered fourteen listings, counted them, then wrote the limits section under the findings, as the last thing.

**What I wrote:** That the sample was small and from one window. Both true, and both chosen because the findings had already survived them.

**What I left out:** That every employer in the sample has a public careers page, which may mean a particular kind of company: larger, more formal, perhaps more used to hiring from other countries.

**Why that one was missing:** Six of my fourteen were remote. Naming the public-board bias would have said that my most interesting finding is partly an artefact of how I gathered.

**What writing them first would have done:** The bias is obvious before you have a result to protect. Written in advance it is a method note; written afterwards it is a concession you are reluctant to make.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is writing limits at the end, because that is where they appear in the document. Once you know your findings, the limits you write are the ones your findings survive.

**Trade-off:** Limits written first are sometimes wrong about what will matter, and two of mine turned out to bound nothing.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how different companies without public boards are. Nothing in this method could reach them, which is the point of saying so.

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Somebody must know what the hiring market wants. Why does this course not tell you?

- Its own evidence is two dated captures, and a conclusion drawn from two would be invented.
- Hiring differs so much between countries and cities that no course could generalise about it.
- The market moves so quickly that anything taught now would be out of date within months.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Its own evidence is two dated captures, and a conclusion drawn from two would be invented. — Two captures from one day is not a study. Many people will tell you confidently what the market wants; few can cite a dated, multi-employer sample, and the ones who can will also tell you how narrow it is.

Hiring differs so much between countries and cities that no course could generalise about it. — Often true, and not the reason given here. The reason is that this course does not hold the evidence, which is also why you gather your own.

The market moves so quickly that anything taught now would be out of date within months. — It does change quickly, which is why your own sample expires. The absence here is of evidence rather than of currency.

Improve: Write what the two captures do and do not establish in step 1. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Your account adds nothing the course’s own note does not say.

Answers to revisit: gap-states, gap-cannot, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

A job site says four thousand remote design roles are open. Can you cite that?

- As a rough indication of demand, rounded down to allow for duplicates and reposts.
- As evidence of demand, once the site, the search words and the date are written beside it.
- As a fact about one site’s search only: it counts duplicates, reposts and expired listings.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

As a rough indication of demand, rounded down to allow for duplicates and reposts. — Rounding down guesses at an error nobody has measured, for a number that may count one job several times.

As evidence of demand, once the site, the search words and the date are written beside it. — Naming the source dates the number without giving it a denominator anybody can check.

As a fact about one site’s search only: it counts duplicates, reposts and expired listings. — Two sites give two different totals for the same day. Use an aggregator to collect employer names, then go to the employer’s own page for anything you will cite as evidence.

Improve: Write the source rule in step 3, separating finding from citing. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Your rule lets you use an aggregator without citing one.

Answers to revisit: source-rule, aggregator-use, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 3 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-3.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Why write the sample’s limits before gathering rather than after?

- Limits written first take less time, because nothing has to be reread or rechecked later.
- Once findings exist, the limits you choose tend to be the ones your findings survive.
- A plan should be complete before gathering starts, and limits are part of a complete plan.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Limits written first take less time, because nothing has to be reread or rechecked later. — It takes about the same time either way. The difference is what you are willing to write once you know your findings.

Once findings exist, the limits you choose tend to be the ones your findings survive. — The public-board bias is obvious before you have a result to protect. Written in advance it is a method note; written afterwards it is a concession you are reluctant to make.

A plan should be complete before gathering starts, and limits are part of a complete plan. — Tidy and true, and it misses the reason: what you know when you write them changes what you write.

Improve: Write the limits in step 4 now, before any gathering. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: At least one limit would weaken a finding you would like to have.

Answers to revisit: cannot-show, expiry-rule, improvement-made

</details>


### Name the advice test

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-brief.

A question you will ask of any career advice you are given.

- Write the question you will ask of any career advice you receive.
- Save the plan and the limits.

**Start here:** Write the question in a form you would actually say to somebody senior.

**Enough:** Your test asks for a source, a count and a date.

**The advice test:** What dated source is this from, how many employers, and when. Most advice fails it, which is information about the advice rather than about you.

**Failing the test:** Not the same as being wrong. It means the claim is opinion, and opinion from somebody experienced is still worth hearing as opinion.

**Repair:** The one change a Check question asks you to make. Make it in the step it belongs to, then record here that you made it.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-sort-1.

Six made up pieces of career advice a learner might be given. For each one, decide what it is.

“Eleven of the fourteen listings I captured between the second and the thirteenth of March asked for a portfolio.”

- cites a dated source
- opinion — useful, uncited
- a number about the aggregator

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

cites a dated source — A count, a sample size and a window. Narrow, checkable, and the only kind of claim this module teaches you to make.

opinion — useful, uncited — It names its source precisely.

a number about the aggregator — The listings were captured from employers’ own pages.

Now write your own advice test as a question you can ask out loud without sounding combative.

</details>

opinion — useful, uncited — Probably drawn from real experience and worth hearing as that. In my own fourteen, three named a specific tool and one named four interchangeable ones.

a number about the aggregator — No number is given at all.

### Try the distinction · 3 of 6

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-sort-3.

Six made up pieces of career advice a learner might be given. For each one, decide what it is.

“There are four thousand remote design jobs open right now — I searched.”

- cites a dated source
- opinion — useful, uncited
- a number about the aggregator

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

cites a dated source — A search total is undated in practice and counts duplicates, reposts and expired listings.

opinion — useful, uncited — It presents itself as a measurement, which is what makes it worse than an opinion.

a number about the aggregator — It measures what one site indexed under one query. Two sites give two different numbers for the same day.

Now write your own advice test as a question you can ask out loud without sounding combative.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-sort-4.

Six made up pieces of career advice a learner might be given. For each one, decide what it is.

“Companies abroad will not hire from India at junior level.”

- cites a dated source
- opinion — useful, uncited
- a number about the aggregator

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

cites a dated source — No employer, date or count is offered.

opinion — useful, uncited — Often said, sometimes by people with real experience of being turned down. In my fourteen, two said they can employ people based in India, one required the right to work in the UK, and eleven said nothing about who they can hire.

a number about the aggregator — There is no number.

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-sort-5.

Six made up pieces of career advice a learner might be given. For each one, decide what it is.

“Of the fourteen listings I captured between the second and the thirteenth of March, two said they can employ people based in India and eleven said nothing about who they can hire.”

- cites a dated source

cites a dated source — Counts out of a stated, dated sample, including the eleven silences, which are the most useful part.

### Try the distinction · 6 of 6

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-sort-6.

Six made up pieces of career advice a learner might be given. For each one, decide what it is.

“The going rate for a junior designer is about this much — that is what the salary site shows.”

- cites a dated source
- opinion — useful, uncited
- a number about the aggregator

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

cites a dated source — Salary sites aggregate self-reported figures with no stated sample or window.

opinion — useful, uncited — It borrows the authority of a number it cannot account for.

a number about the aggregator — It describes who reported to that site, which is a particular and unknown group. Nothing in your own sample can check it, since almost no listing states pay.

Now write your own advice test as a question you can ask out loud without sounding combative.

</details>

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: Your neighbour is deciding whether to pay for a course to become a bicycle mechanic. A shop owner told him “there’s huge demand, every shop is short of mechanics”, and a classifieds site shows 340 results for “bike mechanic” in his city today. He asks whether that settles it.

**Task:** Decide what each of the two claims can and cannot support, and name one dated check he could make before paying. Explain why.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Treats the shop owner’s remark or the 340 results as evidence of demand, or dismisses both without suggesting any check.
- Adequate: Calls the remark an experienced opinion and the 340 a number about one site’s search (duplicates, reposts, expired ads), and suggests gathering dated listings from shops’ own pages or noticeboards.
- Strong: Adequate, plus writes the limits before gathering — a dozen listings cannot show demand or pay, and they expire — and notes the owner may be describing his own shop, which is worth asking about as opinion.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

Optional: may be left empty.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: What this module refuses to tell you
What I am trying to do: Understand why this course teaches no conclusions about the hiring market, and set up the evidence method you will run yourself.

Key idea or terms:
Two dated captures: Two vacancies, retrieved on two dates. Evidence that those employers published those words on those days, and nothing wider.
Not a study: A sample of two, not designed, not representative of anything. The course says so rather than rounding it up into guidance.
A window: A fixed period, about two weeks, in which everything is gathered. It makes the sample datable and stops it drifting across months.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Writing an evidence plan, and writing it after starting to gather. Opened career pages and started copying anything that looked relevant into a document, meaning to tidy it up once I saw what was there.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “What the course’s two vacancy captures do establish” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “What the course’s two vacancy captures do establish”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

**Continue without AI:** Stay in this course and use the first “Try the distinction” question. Choose an answer, read the explanation, then return to “What the course’s two vacancy captures do establish” and write one sentence in your own words.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Read the recorded vacancy note and restate it without adding conclusions.
- Write the plan before gathering anything.

- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — How one employer states responsibilities, levels and its hiring process. Purpose: Gives a single worked example of what an employer publishes, before you gather your own. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer, and a remote-first US-headquartered one; it is explicitly not a representative India hiring study and no market conclusion may be drawn from it. Fallback: R22.
- R37: [NN/g: confidence intervals and margins of error](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/confidence-interval/) — How sample size and variability limit what a proportion can support. Purpose: Grounds the rule against computing rates from a dozen vacancies. Free article, no account; the site sells courses and reports that are not required. Verified 2026-09-06. Written for usability metrics; the article gives no formulas, so show your inputs if you calculate anything. Fallback: R45.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**The course's own limit is stated accurately**

Adequate evidence: A written account of the two captures and what they cannot support.

0 — Not stated, or overstated.

1 — Stated vaguely.

2 — Accurate about the sample and its limits.

3 — As adequate, and it notes the expiry rate as the real finding.

Repair: Read the recorded vacancy note and restate it without adding conclusions. Recheck: The statement.

**An evidence plan names sources, fields and a window**

Adequate evidence: A plan with a target count, source rule, field list and date window.

0 — No plan.

1 — A target count only.

2 — Sources, fields and window all named.

3 — As adequate, and eligibility, work location and working hours are three separate fields.

Repair: Write the plan before gathering anything. Recheck: The plan.

**Aggregator counts are excluded as evidence**

Adequate evidence: A written source rule distinguishing finding from citing.

0 — Aggregators treated as evidence.

1 — Excluded without explanation.

2 — Excluded with the reason.

3 — As adequate, and the permitted use of aggregators is defined.

Repair: Write why an aggregator total is a number about the aggregator. Recheck: The source rule.

**The sample's limits are written before gathering**

Adequate evidence: A pre-written statement of what the sample cannot show, plus expiry.

0 — No limits stated.

1 — Written afterwards.

2 — Written in advance.

3 — As adequate, and it names when the sample would need re-gathering.

Repair: Write the limits now, before the first vacancy is recorded. Recheck: The limits statement.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson gathers the evidence by hand.

**Review criteria:**

- The course's own limit is stated accurately
- An evidence plan names sources, fields and a window
- Aggregator counts are excluded as evidence
- The sample's limits are written before gathering

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The course's own catalog records what happened when it tried to gather hiring evidence on 6 September 2026: eight vacancy URLs attempted, one full capture, one partial capture, six listings already expired to a board index, and one host refusing the request. That is nine outcomes for eight URLs, and the record does not say which URL had two, so an honest restatement keeps the mismatch visible rather than tidying it away. The expiry is the most useful thing the attempt produced, because it tells you vacancy evidence decays within weeks and has to be gathered fresh by whoever needs it.
- Two captures cannot establish demand, salary, prevalence, remote eligibility or what a junior role expects in India. So this module teaches none of those things. What it teaches is the method: which sources count, what to record, how to compare, and what the resulting sample can and cannot support — the same discipline the research modules applied to participants.
- Employers' own boards are the evidence. An aggregator's count of “design jobs in Bengaluru” is a number about the aggregator, mixing duplicates, expired posts and reposts; it is not employer evidence and cannot be cited as such. The rule is the same one you applied to resources: go to the source, record the date, record what it actually says.
- A vacancy is evidence that on a stated date an employer published a stated set of expectations. It is not evidence that the role is open, that it will be filled, that the expectations are real rather than aspirational, or that anyone in your position would be considered. Holding that distinction is what stops a career search becoming a series of private conclusions about your own worth.
- Decide now to record three things separately for every listing, because one sentence in a listing usually answers only one of them. Country eligibility is who the employer says it can hire and from where, including work authorisation and visa sponsorship. Work location is where the work happens: remote, hybrid with a named city and number of office days, or on-site. Working hours are any required overlap or core hours, converted to Indian Standard Time with the date you converted them, because Europe and the United States change their clocks and India does not. “Remote” answers the second question and says nothing about the first or the third.

[GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/).

</details>

## Lesson 2: Gathering the vacancy evidence by hand

Stable ID: m20-l02-v1. Core.

This is the only hiring evidence you will have, and nobody else is going to gather it for you.

Bring: The evidence plan and its field list.

Starting route: Recommended route: Build a folder of dated text copies, one per listing in a folder on your own computer, then record what you did and what you saw in the worksheet here so it is saved and reviewable. The files stay with you; nothing is uploaded. Alternative route: Prefer to keep your notes in the same folder as the files? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter, then note the folder in Your work.

- At least twelve captured listings with all planned fields
- Verbatim requirements for each
- A recorded list of failures with reasons and dates
- Local text copies of every capture

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: An employer list, with aggregators used for names rather than numbers.

**Words you will use**

- **Employer list first:** Names before listings. It stops the sample being whatever one site chose to show you that morning.
- **Careers page:** The employer’s own, usually at their domain. It is where the current wording lives.
- **Verbatim:** The employer’s words, copied. Summarising is interpretation, and doing it at capture time means you can never check what was actually said.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Building a list of employers, and capturing from the aggregator instead. Captured twelve listings straight from the board, because they were there, formatted alike and quick to copy.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “The employers you will check, and how you found each”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Gather at least twelve dated vacancies from employers' own boards, recording the fields your plan named, and record every failure.


### Go to the employer's own careers page, not the aggregator's copy

Go to the employer's own careers page, not the aggregator's copy.

Copy the stated requirements verbatim; do not summarise yet.


### Record every failure: expired, blocked, or no longer listed

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Record every failure: expired, blocked, or no longer listed.


### Save a local copy; the page will be gone in weeks

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Over eleven days, twenty-six employer career pages were opened and fourteen listings captured. Recorded: employer, direct URL, retrieval date, exact title, stated years of experience, and three separate fit fields — who the employer says it can hire and from where, where the work happens (remote, or the city and office days), and any required hours with the same hours in IST. Where a listing said nothing on one of the three, the field says not stated. Failures recorded: seven listings had expired to a board index between finding and capture, three companies had no public careers page, and two pages would not load without an account, which was refused. Every captured listing was saved as text with its date at the top.


### Choose where you will do the work

Recommended route: Build a folder of dated text copies, one per listing in a folder on your own computer, then record what you did and what you saw in the worksheet here so it is saved and reviewable. The files stay with you; nothing is uploaded. Alternative route: Prefer to keep your notes in the same folder as the files? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter, then note the folder in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Build the employer list

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

An employer list, with aggregators used for names rather than numbers.

- Use aggregators only to collect employer names.
- Find each employer's own careers page.

**Start here:** Collect twenty or more employer names before opening a single listing.

**Enough:** Every name on your list has a company page you can reach.

**Employer list first:** Names before listings. It stops the sample being whatever one site chose to show you that morning.

**Careers page:** The employer’s own, usually at their domain. It is where the current wording lives.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Building a list of employers, and capturing from the aggregator instead.

**What I did:** Captured twelve listings straight from the board, because they were there, formatted alike and quick to copy.

**What I found when I checked three against the employer:** One had been filled and taken down a week earlier. One had a different title on the company’s own page. One was posted by a recruitment agency and the employer was not named at all.

**What that did to the sample:** Three of twelve were not what I had recorded, and I only knew because I checked. The other nine are unchecked in the same way.

**What the agency listing meant:** No employer, so nothing to attribute the requirements to. It is a listing about a listing.

**What I did instead:** Used the board to collect twenty-six employer names, then opened each company’s own page. Slower, and every row now has a direct URL somebody could open.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is capturing from the aggregator because the listings are right there and consistently formatted. That consistency is the aggregator’s, not the employers’, and it hides staleness, retitling and listings with no employer at all.

**Trade-off:** Going employer by employer took four evenings rather than one, and three companies had no public page at all.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how many of the twenty-six were hiring but not publishing. Nothing in this method can see them.

### Capture

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

A capture table with every planned field filled, and the requirements copied verbatim.

- Record the planned fields for each listing.
- Copy the requirements verbatim.
- Save a dated local text copy.

**Start here:** Fill every field for the first listing before starting the second.

**Enough:** No row has a blank you would have to guess at later.

**Verbatim:** The employer’s words, copied. Summarising is interpretation, and doing it at capture time means you can never check what was actually said.

**Retrieval date:** The day you took the copy. Without it the capture cannot be dated, and an undated vacancy is worth very little.


### Your capture table: one row per listing, every planned field filled

Employer, URL, retrieval date, title and stated seniority. Eligibility, work location and hours get their own answers in step 4.

### The requirements, copied word for word rather than summarised

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-verbatim-requirements.

Summarising now loses the wording, and the wording is the evidence.

**Answer:** The requirements, copied word for word rather than summarised

Summarising now loses the wording, and the wording is the evidence.


### Record the failures

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

Every failure recorded with a reason and a date, and the rate of pages opened to listings captured.

- Note expired, missing, blocked and account-gated listings.
- Give each a date and a reason.

**Start here:** Write the failures as they happen rather than reconstructing them at the end.

**Enough:** Your record says how many pages produced how many listings.

**Failure rate:** Pages opened against listings captured, with the failures between them. It is the honest denominator; in the made-up example, fourteen were captured from twenty-six opened.

**Account-gated:** A listing that will not load without signing up. This course records it as a refusal rather than treating it as an obstacle to work around.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-demo.

Made-up example. Recording what the gathering cost, and recording only what worked.

**What my table held:** Fourteen complete rows. A clean piece of work, and it reads as though fourteen searches produced fourteen listings.

**What actually happened:** Twenty-six employer pages opened. Seven listings expired between finding and capture, three companies had no public careers page, and two demanded an account.

**Why the missing twelve matter:** Vacancies expiring within days is itself a finding about how fast this evidence goes stale. It is arguably more useful than anything in the fourteen.

**What the two gated ones meant:** Both were large companies. Recording the refusal says what my method cannot reach, rather than quietly leaving a hole.

**What the record became:** Fourteen captured from twenty-six attempted, with the reasons. Anybody reading it knows what the sample is made of.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is recording only successful captures, because the failures feel like your own inefficiency rather than data. The failure rate is what tells a reader how the sample was selected and how quickly it decays.

**Trade-off:** A stated rate of fourteen from twenty-six looks less impressive than fourteen listings, and it is the same fourteen listings.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether the expired seven differed systematically from the captured fourteen. They vanished before I recorded anything about them.

### Check the three fit fields

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

Each listing’s eligibility, work location and hours recorded separately in its own words, with silences recorded as silences.

- For each listing, record eligibility, work location and hours separately, in the listing's own words.
- Where one of the three is not stated, record that rather than inferring it from the others.

**Start here:** Fill all three fields for one listing before moving to the next, copying the listing’s words.

**Enough:** For every listing, each of the three fields holds the listing’s words or says not stated.

**Stated eligibility:** Words in the listing about who may be employed and from where, including work authorisation and visa sponsorship. Often missing, and worth copying exactly when present.

**Work location:** Where the work happens: remote, hybrid with a named city and number of office days, or on-site. Hybrid alone tells you nothing, so record the city and the days.

**Time-zone overlap:** Hours you must share with a team elsewhere. Convert them to IST with the date, because Europe and the United States move their clocks and India does not.

**Silence:** Many listings say nothing on one or more of the three. Silence is neither permission nor refusal, and it must not be recorded as either.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-1.

Six made up lines from captured listings. For each one, decide what it lets you write in the country-eligibility field: who they can hire, and from where.

“We can hire people in India, Brazil and Poland as contractors; elsewhere we employ through an employer-of-record partner.”

- states eligibility
- states nothing — record the silence
- your inference, not theirs

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

states eligibility — Specific, and it names the arrangement as well as the countries. Copy it exactly, including the word contractors.

states nothing — record the silence — It names India directly as a country they can hire in.

your inference, not theirs — No inference is needed; the countries are in their words.

Now go through your own rows: for each listing, check that eligibility, location and hours each hold the listing’s words or say not stated.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-2.

Six made up lines from captured listings. For each one, decide what it lets you write in the country-eligibility field: who they can hire, and from where.

“We are a remote-first company.”

- states eligibility
- states nothing — record the silence
- your inference, not theirs

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

states eligibility — Remote-first describes how the company works, not who it may employ or from where.

states nothing — record the silence — It describes working arrangements, which belong in the location field if the role itself is remote. About who they can hire it says nothing.

your inference, not theirs — Recording it as silence is the opposite of inferring from it.

Now go through your own rows: for each listing, check that eligibility, location and hours each hold the listing’s words or say not stated.

- states eligibility
- states nothing — record the silence

states eligibility — The listing said remote, which is about where the work happens.

states nothing — record the silence — The listing is silent on eligibility; the row is not, because you filled the silence.

your inference, not theirs — A common error. In a sample like the made-up fourteen it would turn eleven silences into eleven yeses.

### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-4.

Six made up lines from captured listings. For each one, decide what it lets you write in the country-eligibility field: who they can hire, and from where.

“Hybrid — three days a week in our Bengaluru office.”

- states eligibility
- states nothing — record the silence
- your inference, not theirs

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

states eligibility — It settles where you must be three days a week, which is the location field. Who they are able to hire there is a separate question this line does not answer.

states nothing — record the silence — It states the work location precisely: Bengaluru, three office days a week. About who they can hire it says nothing, so the eligibility field records not stated.

your inference, not theirs — Nothing is inferred yet. Writing “India only” in the eligibility field would be; that reading belongs in your inference list.

- states eligibility
- states nothing — record the silence

states eligibility — It is checkable against where you are, and it is about hours, not about who may be hired. A company can hire in India and still need European hours.

states nothing — record the silence — It states working hours precisely, and they go in the hours field, converted to IST with the date. About who they can hire it says nothing.

your inference, not theirs — The overlap requirement is theirs; whether you can meet it is a separate line in your own notes.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: You are comparing evening cookery classes for a cousin. A listings site shows twelve. On the schools’ own pages, three classes no longer exist, one school needs an account to show dates, and two prices differ from the listings site.

**Task:** Decide what goes in your record for each problem and which figures count as captured, then explain why you chose that source as the evidence.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps the listings site’s details because they are quicker, or records only the classes that worked, with no failures and no source.
- Adequate: Captures eight from the schools’ own pages, using the school’s price where it differs; records four failures (three gone, one account-gated) with dates; reports eight captured from twelve attempted.
- Strong: Adequate, plus treats the price mismatches as a sign the listings site is stale, saves a dated copy of each school page, and says what the eight cannot show, such as whether places are still free.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

Optional: may be left empty.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Gathering the vacancy evidence by hand
What I am trying to do: Gather at least twelve dated vacancies from employers' own boards, recording the fields your plan named, and record every failure.

Key idea or terms:
Employer list first: Names before listings. It stops the sample being whatever one site chose to show you that morning.
Careers page: The employer’s own, usually at their domain. It is where the current wording lives.
Verbatim: The employer’s words, copied. Summarising is interpretation, and doing it at capture time means you can never check what was actually said.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Building a list of employers, and capturing from the aggregator instead. Captured twelve listings straight from the board, because they were there, formatted alike and quick to copy.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “The employers you will check, and how you found each” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — The structure of a published role: responsibilities, requirements and process. Purpose: Shows what fields a well-written listing contains, before you compare many. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer; treat it as a specimen of format, never as the market's expectations. Fallback: R22.
- R48: [GitLab job families: product design management](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-families/product/product-design-management/) — How levels, scope and expectations are described across a ladder. Purpose: Gives published vocabulary for reading seniority claims in listings. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. Management levels at one company; its ladder is its own and does not define seniority elsewhere. Fallback: R23.

<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**Captures come from employers' own pages**

Adequate evidence: Direct employer URLs for every captured listing.

0 — Aggregator listings used.

1 — Mixed sources.

2 — All from employer pages.

3 — As adequate, and aggregators were used only to find names.

Repair: Re-capture any aggregator listing from the employer's own page. Recheck: The URL list.

**All planned fields are recorded, verbatim where required**

Adequate evidence: A complete row per listing with requirements quoted and eligibility, location and hours as separate entries.

0 — Fields missing or summarised.

1 — Most fields present, or the three fit fields merged into one.

2 — All fields, requirements verbatim, the three fit fields kept separate.

3 — As adequate, and interpretation was deferred to the next lesson.

Repair: Return to the saved copies and complete the fields. Recheck: The capture table.

**Failures are recorded with reasons and dates**

Adequate evidence: A failure list covering expiry, absence and gating.

0 — Failures not recorded.

1 — Counted without reasons.

2 — Recorded with reasons and dates.

3 — As adequate, and the attempted total is reported beside the captured total.

Repair: Reconstruct the failures honestly and record what you can. Recheck: The failure list.

**Local dated copies exist for every capture**

Adequate evidence: Text copies with retrieval dates.

0 — No copies.

1 — Some copies.

2 — A dated copy per capture.

3 — As adequate, and the copies are stored where they will survive.

Repair: Save the text of each listing with its date today. Recheck: The saved copies.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson compares the listings without inventing a market.

**Review criteria:**

- Captures come from employers' own pages
- All planned fields are recorded, verbatim where required
- Failures are recorded with reasons and dates
- Local dated copies exist for every capture

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- Aggregators are useful for finding employer names and useless as evidence, because their copy of a listing may be stale, edited or duplicated. Once you have a name, go to that company's own careers page and take the listing from there, which is where any current statement about eligibility, location and hours will be.
- The fields matter because comparison later depends on them. Seniority as stated, not as you interpret it. Then three fit fields that are easy to merge and must stay apart: country eligibility (who the employer says it can hire, from which countries, and whether it sponsors visas), work location (remote, hybrid with the city and office days, or on-site and the city), and working hours (any required overlap or core hours, with the same hours in IST). A listing that says remote has answered the second and said nothing about the first or the third. Verbatim requirements now, analysis in the next lesson.
- Failures are data. When six of eight URLs have already expired, that is the strongest single fact about vacancy evidence and it belongs in your record with the date. It also protects you from a false conclusion later, when a thin sample looks like thin demand rather than like fast expiry.
- Save a local copy of each listing — text is enough. In a month you will want to check what a listing actually said, and the page will very likely be gone. This is the same discipline as the course's own catalog: record what you saw and when you saw it.

[GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/).

</details>

## Lesson 3: Reading vacancies without inventing a market

Stable ID: m20-l03-v1. Core.

The comparison is useful for preparing and dangerous for concluding, and the difference is entirely in how you write it.

Bring: At least twelve captured listings.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A comparison written as counts out of your sample
- Contradictions between listings kept and named
- Stated requirements separated from your inferences
- A written limits statement for the comparison

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: Each requirement as a count out of your sample, the three fit fields counted separately, with the size and window beside them.

**Words you will use**

- **A count out of a sample:** Nine of fourteen. It carries its own denominator, so nobody can mistake it for a rate across an industry.
- **Why not percentages:** Sixty-four per cent of fourteen implies a sample nobody had. Module 15 made the same point about three participants.
- **A kept contradiction:** Two listings that want opposite things, reported as two listings wanting opposite things. It is information about how varied these jobs are.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Writing up a vacancy comparison, and writing it in percentages. Seventy-nine per cent of listings require a portfolio and sixty-four per cent want process case studies. It looked like a finding.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Each requirement as a count out of your sample”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Compare your captured listings honestly — as counts out of your sample, with contradictions kept — and write what the comparison cannot support.


### Idea 1: At this size, report counts out of your sample, not percentage…

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

At this size, report counts out of your sample, not percentages: a percentage of fourteen implies a sample you do not have.


### Say “nine of fourteen listings I captured”, with the dates

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-2.

Say “nine of fourteen listings I captured”, with the dates.


### Keep contradictions; listings disagree and that is information

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-3.

Keep contradictions; listings disagree and that is information.


### Idea 4: Separate stated requirements from implied ones, and count elig…

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Separate stated requirements from implied ones, and count eligibility, work location and hours separately.


### Write what the comparison cannot support before using it

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-5.

Write what the comparison cannot support before using it.


### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Fourteen listings, gathered over eleven days. Counts recorded: eleven of fourteen ask for a portfolio; nine ask for case studies showing process; six state a years-of-experience minimum, ranging from two to five; four mention accessibility explicitly; three name a specific design tool and one names four interchangeable ones. The fit fields are counted separately, each adding up to fourteen: eligibility — two say they can employ people based in India, one requires the right to work in the UK, eleven say nothing; work location — six remote, five hybrid with a named city, three on-site; hours — four state an overlap or core hours, ten say nothing. Contradiction kept: three listings expect the designer to run research, two say research is handled by a research team. Limits written: English-language listings from employers with public boards, one window, no evidence about pay, demand, or these employers' actual practice.


### Choose where you will do the work

Section: learn. Stable action: workspace.

Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Count

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

Each requirement as a count out of your sample, the three fit fields counted separately, with the size and window beside them.

- Count how many listings state each requirement.
- Write every count as a number out of your sample size.
- Count eligibility, work location and hours as three separate sets, each adding up to your sample with silences included.

**Start here:** Write every finding with its denominator in the same sentence.

**Enough:** No finding in your writing is a percentage.

**A count out of a sample:** Nine of fourteen. It carries its own denominator, so nobody can mistake it for a rate across an industry.

**Why not percentages:** Sixty-four per cent of fourteen implies a sample nobody had. Module 15 made the same point about three participants.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Writing up a vacancy comparison, and writing it in percentages.

**What I wrote:** Seventy-nine per cent of listings require a portfolio and sixty-four per cent want process case studies. It looked like a finding.

**What the numbers were:** Eleven of fourteen, and nine of fourteen. Two decimal-free percentages standing in for two small counts.

**What the percentage did:** Removed the denominator. Seventy-nine per cent reads as a property of the hiring market; eleven of fourteen reads as a property of my fourteen listings.

**What happened when somebody asked:** Seventy-nine per cent of what? The honest answer is fourteen listings from employers with public boards, gathered over eleven days, and that answer undoes the sentence entirely.

**What I write now:** Eleven of the fourteen listings I captured between the second and the thirteenth of March asked for a portfolio. Longer, and it survives the question.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is converting small counts to percentages, because percentages look professional and comparable. They hide the denominator, which is the only thing that tells a reader how much weight the number holds.

**Trade-off:** Counts with their window attached make every sentence longer, and repeating the window gets tiresome by the fourth one.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether eleven of fourteen would hold across a different fortnight. Nothing here can say, which is why the dates are in the sentence.


### Each requirement as a count out of your sample

### Keep the contradictions

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

Requirements the listings disagree about, kept as disagreements, with what each tells you.

- Find requirements that listings disagree about.
- Record the disagreement rather than averaging it.

**Start here:** Look for requirements where two listings want opposite things.

**Enough:** At least one disagreement is recorded rather than averaged.

**A kept contradiction:** Two listings that want opposite things, reported as two listings wanting opposite things. It is information about how varied these jobs are.

**Averaging:** Turning a disagreement into a middle position nobody stated. It produces a job description no employer wrote.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Handling listings that disagree, and resolving them into one picture.

**What I found:** Three listings expected the designer to run their own research. Two said research was handled by a research team and the designer worked from their findings.

**What I wrote:** That employers generally expect some research involvement. A sentence both groups could be squeezed into.

**What that sentence describes:** No job. Neither group wants some involvement: one wants somebody who can recruit and interview, the other wants somebody who can work from a research team’s synthesis.

**What keeping the split gave me:** Two different applications. For the first group, the interviews and the synthesis are the evidence; for the second, the decision record showing how I used somebody else’s findings.

**Why the disagreement was the finding:** It says these are two different jobs with one title, which is more useful for preparing than any consensus I could have manufactured.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is resolving the disagreement into a general statement, because a single picture feels like the point of comparing. The average describes a job nobody advertised, and it hides the choice you actually have to make.

**Trade-off:** Two pictures mean preparing two emphases rather than one, which is more work with the same portfolio.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: which group is more common overall. Three against two in fourteen listings settles nothing.


### Requirements the listings disagree about, recorded as disagreements

Write your answer for “Requirements the listings disagree about, recorded as disagreements”. Use the task instructions below to decide what to include.

**Answer:** Requirements the listings disagree about, recorded as disagreements

### Separate inference

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

Stated requirements separated from your inferences, with the inferences marked as yours.

- Mark which requirements were stated and which you inferred.
- Keep inferences in a separate list.

**Start here:** Move anything not written in a listing into the inference list.

**Enough:** Every line in the stated list could be quoted from a capture.

**Stated:** Written in the listing, in their words. Everything else belongs in the other list.

**Inferred:** Reasonable, yours, and often right. Marked as yours so nobody reports it back as something an employer said.

### Write the limits

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

A limits statement naming who the method excluded, with the window and expiry.

- State who your sample excludes and what it cannot show.
- Include the gathering window and expiry.

**Start here:** Name the employers your method could never have seen.

**Enough:** The statement covers exclusion, scope and expiry.

**Who you could not reach:** Employers without public boards, listings in languages you do not read, and everybody hiring through networks. It is most of the hiring that happens.

**Expiry:** The date this comparison stops describing anything current. Weeks rather than months.

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

You write that seventy-nine per cent of listings require a portfolio. Eleven of your fourteen did. What should the sentence say?

- Seventy-nine per cent, with a footnote giving the sample size and the dates of the window.
- Eleven of the fourteen I captured, with the dates, so the denominator travels with the claim.
- Seventy-nine per cent, since eleven divided by fourteen is 0.786 and rounding it is honest arithmetic.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Seventy-nine per cent, with a footnote giving the sample size and the dates of the window. — A footnote is not in the sentence, and the sentence is what gets quoted.

Eleven of the fourteen I captured, with the dates, so the denominator travels with the claim. — Seventy-nine per cent of what? The honest answer — fourteen listings, public boards, eleven days — undoes the sentence. Eleven of fourteen carries its own answer.

Seventy-nine per cent, since eleven divided by fourteen is 0.786 and rounding it is honest arithmetic. — The arithmetic is right and the implication is not: a percentage reads as a property of the hiring market rather than of your fourteen listings.

Improve: Rewrite every finding in step 1 as a count with its window in the same sentence. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: No finding in your writing is a percentage.

Answers to revisit: counts-written, sample-window, fit-counts, improvement-made

</details>

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

The majority position, three to two, with the minority mentioned as an exception. — Three against two in fourteen settles nothing, and the minority is a real kind of job rather than an exception.

Both, kept as a disagreement: they describe two different jobs that share one title. — Some research involvement describes no job. Keeping the split gives you two applications: interviews and synthesis for one group, a decision record using somebody else’s findings for the other.

That employers generally expect some research involvement, which covers all five listings. — A sentence both groups squeeze into and neither wants, describing a job nobody advertised.

Improve: Record the disagreement and what it tells you in step 2. Note the change in step 5.

The counts — six remote, two stating India, eleven silent — describe your fourteen and point in no direction about remote roles in general.

Only two of the fourteen stated India explicitly, so rarely is a fair summary of this sample. — Two stating it and eleven saying nothing is not evidence that the eleven would refuse, and remote is a separate field.

**Sweeping:** Searching for the phrases rather than re-reading. Re-reading finds the ones you would notice anyway.

<summary>After your attempt</summary>

reportable as written — Rarely is a rate across a population you did not sample.

a market claim — rewrite as a count — The nearest counts are six remote listings, and two of fourteen saying they can employ people based in India with eleven saying nothing. Neither supports rarely, and remote is a different field from eligibility.

an inference — move it — Silence is not refusal. This is your reading of eleven silences, and it belongs where a reader can see whose reading it is.

Now search your own writing for the market, employers want and any per cent sign.

</details>

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: A friend copied twenty reviews of a local gym from the gym’s own website over one week. Fifteen mention friendly staff, six complain about crowding at 7 p.m., and two say the showers were cold. She wants to post: “Seventy-five per cent of members love the staff, and evenings are always overcrowded.”

**Task:** Decide how she should rewrite that sentence so it stays true to her twenty reviews, and explain what it cannot claim.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps the percentage or “always”, or treats twenty reviews as describing all members.
- Adequate: Rewrites as counts with the sample and window — fifteen of twenty reviews on the gym’s own site that week mention friendly staff; six mention crowding at 7 p.m. — and drops “members love” and “always”.
- Strong: Adequate, plus names who the sample misses (reviews the gym chose to show, people who never write reviews) and that it dates, and keeps any “members feel” reading in a separate list marked as her inference.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why
