<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: A resume where every line survives a question
What I am trying to do: Write a one-page resume in which every claim maps to an artefact, and check it by having someone challenge each line.

Key idea or terms:
What happened: The outcome line: a count with its period where you measured, and nothing was measured where you did not. Both are better than improved.
One page: What gets read. Everything that matters about three self-directed projects fits, and the second page is where unsupported lines collect.
Tracing: Putting the matrix beside the resume and checking line against cell. It takes twenty minutes and it is what makes the document defensible.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up record from the course’s practice repair-shop project. After the shop changed how it wrote its job slips, the owner’s tally showed eleven progress-chasing calls over the five working days before the change and seven over the five working days after. Nothing else was controlled. The status page you designed was only a demonstration that no customer used. You did the research; the owner kept the tally.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Your entries, in plain structure, on one page” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “Your entries, in plain structure, on one page”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

**Continue without AI:** Stay in this course and use the first “Try a supplied example” question. Choose an answer, read the explanation, then return to “Your entries, in plain structure, on one page” and write one sentence in your own words.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Put the matrix beside the resume and check each line.
- Replace every vague improvement with what was counted, over what period, and by whom.

- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — The language an employer uses for responsibilities. Purpose: Provides published vocabulary for describing what you did without inflating it. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer; borrow the precision, not the seniority language, and never claim its levels. Fallback: R22.
- R66: [W3C: How to Meet WCAG 2.2 (quick reference)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/quickref/) — The text alternative criteria and the guidelines under Understandable, at levels A and AA. Purpose: Explains why an extractable, plainly structured document is the accessible choice. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. Written for web content rather than documents; the reasoning about real text transfers, the criteria are not a document standard. Fallback: R41.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**Every line maps to an artefact**

Adequate evidence: A line-by-line trace to matrix cells.

0 — Untraced claims.

1 — Mostly traced.

2 — Every line traced.

3 — As adequate, and untraceable lines were removed rather than softened.

Repair: Put the matrix beside the resume and check each line. Recheck: The trace.

**Outcomes are counts or honest absences**

Adequate evidence: Counts with their period where measured, plain statements where not.

0 — Improvement language without numbers, or a rate made from small counts.

1 — Some counts, some vague claims, or counts without their period.

2 — Counts with their period, or honest absences, throughout, and no causal verb the evidence cannot carry.

3 — As adequate, and one entry states plainly that nothing was measured.

Repair: Replace every vague improvement with what was counted, over what period, and by whom. Recheck: The entries.

**Project categories are labelled**

Adequate evidence: A category on each project entry.

0 — Unlabelled.

1 — Labelled inconsistently.

2 — All labelled.

3 — As adequate, and nothing implies employment that did not exist.

Repair: Add the category to every entry. Recheck: The labels.

**The challenge pass was run and recorded**

Adequate evidence: A challenge record — someone else's, or a labelled self-review on the solo route — and the resulting changes.

0 — Not run.

1 — Re-read without the challenge questions.

2 — Run with someone, or on the solo route a dated written self-challenge labelled self-review, with changes recorded.

3 — As adequate, and every challenged line ends rewritten, removed, or kept with the artefact that answered the challenge.

Repair: Ask someone to challenge each line and record what you could not defend; with nobody available, run the written questions a day later and label it. Recheck: The change record.

**One line is revised against a captured vacancy**

Adequate evidence: A requirement quoted from your own dated capture, one line before and after, and the artefact behind the after.

0 — No revision, or a template line adopted.

1 — Revised to echo the listing's wording.

2 — Revised to answer the requirement within what the artefact supports, with units and ownership kept.

3 — As adequate, and anything the listing asks that you cannot evidence is left unclaimed and noted.

Repair: Pick one requirement from a capture and rewrite one line using only your own artefact. Recheck: The before and after lines.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson takes the same discipline to what you send an employer.

**Review criteria:**

- Every line maps to an artefact
- Outcomes are counts or honest absences
- Project categories are labelled
- The challenge pass was run and recorded
- One line is revised against a captured vacancy

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The mapping rule does the work. Write the resume, then put the matrix beside it and check each line against a cell. Anything that cannot be traced is either rewritten to what you can show or removed, and on a first draft this pass often catches several lines.
- Lines should say what you did and what happened, with counts where you have them, the period each count covers, and who did what. “Ran five research sessions for a repair shop; after the owner changed how job slips were written, his tally of progress-chasing calls was eleven over five working days before and seven over the five after” is checkable. Notice what it does not say. Not “reduced calls”, which claims the change caused the fall when one uncontrolled comparison of two weeks cannot separate it from an ordinary quieter week. Not “my status page cut calls”, because the status page was only a demonstration that no customer used. Not “eleven to seven a day”, which multiplies both counts by five. Not “cut calls by 36 per cent”, which turns two small counts into a rate. “Improved customer experience through user-centred design” is checkable by nobody.
- Category labelling belongs on the resume as much as in the portfolio. Self-directed projects listed alongside employment, with nothing distinguishing them, is the most common way a resume becomes misleading without a single false sentence in it.
- Format matters for a practical reason: many resumes are read first by software that extracts text, and a design-heavy PDF with text in images extracts as nothing. Plain structure, real text, one page. It is also, incidentally, the accessible choice, which you should be able to explain.
- Then revise against a real listing rather than a template. Take one requirement from a vacancy you captured, with its employer and retrieval date, and rewrite one of your own lines to answer it using only what your artefacts support. If the listing asks for something you cannot evidence, the honest revision leaves it unclaimed rather than borrowing the listing's wording. The challenge pass works best with another person; if nobody is available, put the line-by-line questions to the resume a day later and label the result self-review, because someone else's challenge is still to come.

[GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/).

</details>

## Lesson 7: A public profile, and what does not go on it

Stable ID: m20-l07-v1. Core.

A public profile is permanent, indexed and read by strangers, so what it contains should be a decision rather than a default.

Bring: The resume and published portfolio.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A short profile stating what you do and what you can show
- A written decision about what personal data appears
- Other people appearing only by role or with permission
- A consistency check against the resume

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: An include list and an exclude list for personal information, written before the profile.

**Words you will use**

- **Deciding first:** Writing the lists before the profile, so what appears is a decision rather than whatever the form asked for.
- **Permanent and indexed:** A public profile is copied, cached and searched. Removing something from the page does not remove it from everywhere it was copied to.
- **What you can show:** Named projects with what each evidences. It is the part that separates a profile from a description of a role you would like.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Deciding what a public profile carries, and filling in what the form asked for. Worked down the profile form. Name, photograph, city, phone number, personal email, date of birth, current employer, languages. Every field had a box, so I filled every box.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “The kinds of personal information that will appear, listed by type rather than the details themselves”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Write a public professional profile that says what you do and what you can show, while deciding deliberately what personal information stays off it.


### Say what you do, what you can show, and where to see it

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

Say what you do, what you can show, and where to see it.


### Decide what personal data goes on it before writing

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-2.

Decide what personal data goes on it before writing.


### Do not publish other people's information without permission

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-3.

Do not publish other people's information without permission.


### Keep it consistent with the resume; contradictions get noticed

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Keep it consistent with the resume; contradictions get noticed.


### A profile is not required to be on any particular platform

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-5.

A profile is not required to be on any particular platform.


### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Four sentences: what she does, the three projects and what each shows, the honest note that the work is self-directed practice built during a structured programme, and a link. Personal data decided in advance: city and a controlled email address; no phone number, no address, no personal details. Participants appear as roles; the shop is described as a small repair business without its name, since the owner agreed to the work being shown but not to being identified. The profile was checked line by line against the resume, and one seniority phrase was removed for inconsistency.


### Choose where you will do the work

Section: learn. Stable action: workspace.

Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Decide the data

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

An include list and an exclude list for personal information, written before the profile.

- Write what personal information will and will not appear.
- Default to less; you can add later, not remove from indexes.

**Start here:** Write both lists before opening any profile form.

**Enough:** Nothing appears on your profile that is not on the include list.

**Deciding first:** Writing the lists before the profile, so what appears is a decision rather than whatever the form asked for.

**Permanent and indexed:** A public profile is copied, cached and searched. Removing something from the page does not remove it from everywhere it was copied to.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Deciding what a public profile carries, and filling in what the form asked for.

**What I did:** Worked down the profile form. Name, photograph, city, phone number, personal email, date of birth, current employer, languages. Every field had a box, so I filled every box.

**What I had published:** A phone number and a personal email address on a page strangers can read, and my current employer, who did not know I was looking.

**What happened within a fortnight:** Four recruitment calls to the mobile number and a steady flow of mail to an address I use for everything else.

**What deleting the fields did:** Removed them from the page. The recruitment calls continued for months, because the details had already been copied.

**What the page carries now:** Name, city, and an email address used only for this. The rest is on the resume, which goes to people who ask for it.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is treating the form as the decision, because every field looks required and completeness feels professional. A profile is permanent and indexed, so filling a field is publishing, and publishing is not reversible.

**Trade-off:** A sparse profile gives somebody less reason to believe you exist, and one person did ask for a phone number before a call.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether the city helps or narrows things. It stays because remote listings ask about overlap hours, and a city answers the time-zone question without an address. It says nothing about eligibility, which a listing has to state.

**Answer:** The kinds of personal information that will appear, listed by type rather than the details themselves

### Write the profile

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

A short profile saying what you do, what you can show, and where to see it.

- Say what you do, what you can show, and where.
- Keep it to a few sentences with a link.

**Start here:** Write the three project lines first; the rest of the profile follows from them.

**Enough:** A stranger could tell what you do and what to look at.

**What you can show:** Named projects with what each evidences. It is the part that separates a profile from a description of a role you would like.

**The honest note:** One clause saying the work is self-directed practice. Left out, a reader assumes employment, and finding out later is worse than being told.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-sort-1.

The city you live in.

- fine to publish
- decide deliberately — usually leave it out
- somebody else’s to publish

<summary>After your attempt</summary>

fine to publish — Coarse enough not to locate you, and it answers the overlap-hours question remote listings ask.

decide deliberately — usually leave it out — Worth deciding, and a city is the usual answer where a street address is not.

somebody else’s to publish — It is yours.

Now read your own draft and mark each line as yours to publish or somebody else’s.

</details>

### Check other people

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

Other people appearing by role, with client and business names checked against what was agreed.

- Replace names with roles unless you have permission.
- Check client and employer names against what was agreed.

**Start here:** Go through every proper noun on the page and ask who it belongs to.

**Enough:** Every name on the page is yours or permitted.

**By role:** A shop owner, a screen-reader user, a developer. Specific enough to be useful and not information about a person.

**Agreed to be shown, not to be named:** The commonest shape of a real permission. Work published, identity not.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-demo.

Made-up example. Naming a business on a public profile, and naming it because the owner was pleased.

**What I wrote:** The shop’s name, in the project line, because the owner had been generous and it felt like proper credit.

**What he had agreed to:** The work being shown. We had talked about the page appearing in a portfolio and never about the shop being identified.

**What the profile did that the case study did not:** Made it searchable. The case study is read by people who go looking; a profile line is indexed against the shop’s name.

**What he said when I asked:** That he would rather not, because the project is about how badly the old process worked and his customers can read.

**What the line says now:** A small repair business. It carries the same weight and costs nothing that mattered.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is naming a business as credit, because the instinct is generous and the permission sounds like it covers it. Agreeing to work being shown is not agreeing to be identified, and a profile line is indexed in a way a case study is not.

**Trade-off:** An unnamed business is slightly less convincing to a reader who wants to check the work is real.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether his customers would have minded at all. It is his judgement to make, and he made it.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: A public profile, and what does not go on it
What I am trying to do: Write a public professional profile that says what you do and what you can show, while deciding deliberately what personal information stays off it.

Key idea or terms:
Deciding first: Writing the lists before the profile, so what appears is a decision rather than whatever the form asked for.
Permanent and indexed: A public profile is copied, cached and searched. Removing something from the page does not remove it from everywhere it was copied to.
What you can show: Named projects with what each evidences. It is the part that separates a profile from a description of a role you would like.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Deciding what a public profile carries, and filling in what the form asked for. Worked down the profile form. Name, photograph, city, phone number, personal email, date of birth, current employer, languages. Every field had a box, so I filled every box.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “The kinds of personal information that will appear, listed by type rather than the details themselves” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Cut the adjectives and name the evidence.
- Write the include and exclude lists, then edit the profile to match.

- R54: [GOV.UK: managing research data and participant privacy](https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/user-research/managing-user-research-data-participant-privacy) — Treating information about people as personal data, and what may be shared. Purpose: Governs what you publish about participants, clients and colleagues. Free reading, no account; published 5 November 2018. Verified 2026-09-06. UK GDPR framing, written for research data rather than profiles; the principle transfers, the jurisdiction does not. Fallback: R53.
- R22: [NN/g: UX portfolio](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ux-design-portfolios/) — Prioritising the top three things your portfolio should communicate. Purpose: Shapes a short profile that points at evidence. Free article, no account; the site sells courses and reports that are not required. Verified 2026-09-06. US hiring context. Fallback: R23.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**The profile states what you do and what you can show**

Adequate evidence: A short profile naming the work and linking to evidence.

0 — Adjectives and aspiration.

1 — Descriptive but without evidence.

2 — What you do, what you can show, and where.

3 — As adequate, and a stranger could restate it after one read.

Repair: Cut the adjectives and name the evidence. Recheck: The profile.

**Personal data appears by decision, not default**

Adequate evidence: A written decision about what is included and excluded.

0 — Contact and personal details published unconsidered.

1 — Some restraint, no decision recorded.

2 — A recorded decision.

3 — As adequate, and it defaults to the minimum that lets someone reach you.

Repair: Write the include and exclude lists, then edit the profile to match. Recheck: The decision.

**Other people appear by role or with permission**

Adequate evidence: No unpermitted names of participants, clients or colleagues.

0 — Names published without permission.

1 — Mixed.

2 — Roles or permitted names only.

3 — As adequate, and what each person agreed to is recorded.

Repair: Replace names with roles until permission exists. Recheck: The profile text.

**The profile is consistent with the resume**

Adequate evidence: A line-by-line comparison with disagreements resolved.

0 — Contradictions present.

1 — Broadly consistent, unchecked.

2 — Checked and reconciled.

3 — As adequate, and any claim unsupported in both documents was removed or given its artefact.

Repair: Read them side by side and correct what disagrees. Recheck: The comparison.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson prepares the question you will be asked first.

**Review criteria:**

- The profile states what you do and what you can show
- Personal data appears by decision, not default
- Other people appear by role or with permission
- The profile is consistent with the resume

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The profile has one job: a stranger should learn within a few lines what you do, what evidence exists, and how to reach it. Everything else is optional, and the optional parts are where profiles become long, generic and interchangeable.
- Decide the personal data question first, because it is easier to withhold than to withdraw. Full address, phone number, date of birth, family details, exact employer of a spouse — none of these belong on a public profile, and each has been used to harm people. An email you control and a city are usually enough.
- Other people's data is the part beginners get wrong. Naming a participant, a client who did not agree to be named, or a colleague's role in your project publishes their information as well as yours. The rule from the research modules holds: permission or a role, never a name by default.
- Consistency matters because a reviewer reads both. A profile claiming three years of design work beside a resume showing self-directed projects invites the question you least want. Write the profile from the resume, not from memory.
- Finally, no platform is required. A page on your own portfolio site is a valid public profile. If you use a platform, that is a choice with its own terms, and it should be made deliberately rather than because everyone does.

[GOV.UK: managing research data and participant privacy](https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/user-research/managing-user-research-data-participant-privacy).

</details>

## Lesson 8: The transition story, told without embellishment

Stable ID: m20-l08-v1. Core.

You will be asked this in the first five minutes of every conversation, and an unprepared answer either rambles or overclaims.

Bring: The three projects and your prior work history.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A written ninety-second account, true in every particular
- A statement of what transferred and what did not
- A closing sentence resting on evidence
- Rehearsal timings showing it fits

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: The real reason you moved, with anything shaped to sound inevitable removed.

**Words you will use**

- **What happened:** The actual sequence, including the dull and unflattering parts. Interviewers hear constructed narratives constantly and discount them.
- **Shaped to sound inevitable:** Always been drawn to, realised that, knew it was time. The phrases that turn a series of events into a destiny.
- **Transfer:** Real and worth naming: typography, hierarchy, constraints, finishing. Years of practice at things many people never learn.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Writing the transition account, and writing it as a story of destiny. That I had always been drawn to how people use things, and that graphic design gradually revealed itself as too narrow for that interest.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “The real reason you moved, including the unflattering parts”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Write and rehearse the account of why you moved from graphic design to product design, in a form that is true and takes ninety seconds.


### Tell what happened, not a narrative of destiny

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

Tell what happened, not a narrative of destiny.

Name what your prior work gave you and what it did not.

Ninety seconds; the long version is for when they ask.


### Do not disparage your earlier field or employers

Do not disparage your earlier field or employers.


### End on evidence, not on enthusiasm

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Ninety seconds, four beats. What happened: years of producing material that looked right and being unable to tell whether it worked, and a job where the brief was wrong and nobody had asked anyone. What transferred: typography, hierarchy, working to constraints, finishing. What did not: research, systems, testing her own work, which is what the last two years were for. Where that leaves her: three projects, one with a measured before and after, and a portfolio that states what it does not establish. Rehearsed four times, timed at 105, 95, 88 and 90 seconds, with one phrase removed for sounding rehearsed.


### Choose where you will do the work

Section: learn. Stable action: workspace.

Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Write what actually happened

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

The real reason you moved, with anything shaped to sound inevitable removed.

- Write the real reason, including the unflattering parts.
- Remove anything shaped to sound inevitable.

**Start here:** Write what happened as though to somebody who already knows you.

**Enough:** Nothing in it is there because it sounds good.

**What happened:** The actual sequence, including the dull and unflattering parts. Interviewers hear constructed narratives constantly and discount them.

**Shaped to sound inevitable:** Always been drawn to, realised that, knew it was time. The phrases that turn a series of events into a destiny.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Writing the transition account, and writing it as a story of destiny.

**What I wrote first:** That I had always been drawn to how people use things, and that graphic design gradually revealed itself as too narrow for that interest.

**What actually happened:** Eight years of producing material that looked right with no way of telling whether it worked, and one brief that was plainly wrong which nobody had checked with anybody.

**Why the first version felt safer:** It has no complaint in it, no dissatisfaction, and nothing that could be read as blaming an employer. It is also about nothing.

**What happened when I said the true one aloud:** The listener asked what the wrong brief was, which is a conversation about work. Nobody has ever asked a follow-up question about being drawn to something.

**What I keep:** The eight years, the brief, and that nobody had asked anyone. Three specifics, no destiny, and it takes fifteen seconds.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is smoothing the account into a narrative, because a story feels like what is being asked for. A plain account with specifics stands out precisely because it is unpolished, and specifics are what produce follow-up questions.

**Trade-off:** The true version contains dissatisfaction with previous work, which has to be said without turning into a complaint about employers.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether every interviewer prefers it. One person clearly wanted the tidy version and did not ask anything further.

Boredom, a bad brief, needing more money, being made redundant. All of these are true reasons that people accept. Describe former employers and colleagues without naming them.

### Write the closing

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

A closing sentence resting on what exists, and the question you want it to invite.

- End on what exists rather than on enthusiasm.
- Make it invite a question about your evidence.

**Start here:** End on a thing rather than on a feeling.

**Enough:** Your closing sentence names something somebody could ask about.

**Closing on evidence:** Three projects, one with a measured before and after, and a portfolio that states what it does not establish. It hands the conversation somewhere to go.

**Closing on enthusiasm:** Excited to bring this to a team. It ends the exchange, because there is no question to ask of it.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-1.

Six made up closing sentences for a ninety-second transition account. For each one, decide what it does.

“So I have three projects now, one with a before-and-after measurement, and a portfolio that says what each of them does not establish.”

- rests on evidence
- rests on enthusiasm
- disparages the old field

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

rests on evidence — Names what exists and invites the obvious next question, which is about the measurement.

rests on enthusiasm — There is no feeling in it at all.

disparages the old field — The old field is not mentioned.

Now read your own closing sentence and decide which of the three it is.

</details>

- disparages the old field

### Cut to ninety seconds

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

The account timed and cut to ninety seconds, with the specifics kept.

- Read aloud with a timer and cut until it fits.
- Keep the specifics; cut the transitions.

**Start here:** Read it aloud with a timer before cutting anything.

**Enough:** It fits the time with its specifics intact.

**Ninety seconds:** What the question is worth in a first conversation. The long version is for when they ask for it.

**What to cut:** Transitions, scene-setting and second examples. The counts, the projects and the one thing that went wrong all stay.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-demo.

Made-up example. Cutting the account to ninety seconds, and cutting the details.

**Where I started:** Two minutes and forty seconds, which is a long time to talk without being asked anything.

**What I cut first:** The specifics, because they take longest to say: the eight years, the wrong brief, the call count, the screen-reader session.

**What was left:** Ninety seconds of joining material. I moved from graphic design to product design because I wanted to understand whether my work was effective, and I have been building that understanding since.

**What happened when I said it:** No follow-up question. There was nothing in it to ask about, so the conversation moved on to something else.

**What I cut the second time:** The transitions and the scene-setting. Ninety-five seconds, four specifics intact, and two follow-up questions.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is cutting the specifics, because they are the longest sentences and the joining material feels like the structure. The specifics are the only part that produces a question, and a question is the point of the ninety seconds.

**Trade-off:** Keeping four specifics in ninety seconds means saying them quite plainly, with none of the framing that makes them land gently.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: which specific does the work. Two people asked about the call count and one about the wrong brief.

### Rehearse and check

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-brief.

The account said to somebody — or recorded and played back a day later if nobody is available — with what was heard and anything that sounded rehearsed.

- Say it to someone and ask what they heard; with nobody available, record it, listen back a day later and label it self-review.
- Remove anything that sounded rehearsed or overclaimed.

**Start here:** Ask them to tell you back what they heard rather than whether it was good. Solo: record it, wait a day, and write what a stranger would have heard.

**Enough:** You wrote what was heard rather than whether it was good, labelled as theirs or as your own playback.

**What they heard:** Their account of it, in their words. It is the only way to find out what the ninety seconds actually conveys.

**Sounding rehearsed:** A phrase that arrives too smoothly. It signals a prepared answer, which makes everything after it sound prepared too.

**Repair:** The one change a Check question asks you to make. Make it in the step it belongs to, then record here that you made it.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: Arjun worked five years as a hotel receptionist and wants an operations role at a delivery company. His draft answer to “Why the change?” says he has “always been fascinated by logistics” and that “hotels waste people’s talent”. What happened: he rebuilt the hotel’s luggage-storage log after repeated mix-ups, logged eight mix-ups in the month before and two in the month after, and has never managed a budget or a team.

**Task:** Rewrite the core of his answer and explain what you kept, cut and added.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps the destiny line or the complaint about hotels, or claims his log stopped the mix-ups.
- Adequate: Says what happened (the log he rebuilt), names what transferred and what did not (no budget or team), drops the complaint, and ends on the evidence: eight mix-ups in the month before, two in the month after.
- Strong: Adequate, plus keeps the counts with their periods without claiming the log caused the fall, and stays near ninety seconds by cutting scene-setting rather than specifics.

</details>

<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: The transition story, told without embellishment
What I am trying to do: Write and rehearse the account of why you moved from graphic design to product design, in a form that is true and takes ninety seconds.

Key idea or terms:
What happened: The actual sequence, including the dull and unflattering parts. Interviewers hear constructed narratives constantly and discount them.
Shaped to sound inevitable: Always been drawn to, realised that, knew it was time. The phrases that turn a series of events into a destiny.
Transfer: Real and worth naming: typography, hierarchy, constraints, finishing. Years of practice at things many people never learn.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Writing the transition account, and writing it as a story of destiny. That I had always been drawn to how people use things, and that graphic design gradually revealed itself as too narrow for that interest.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “The real reason you moved, including the unflattering parts” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “The real reason you moved, including the unflattering parts”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

**Continue without AI:** Stay in this course and use the first “Try the distinction” question. Choose an answer, read the explanation, then return to “The real reason you moved, including the unflattering parts” and write one sentence in your own words.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Write what actually happened, then remove what you added to make it neat.
- Name three things your earlier work did not teach you.

- R22: [NN/g: UX portfolio](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ux-design-portfolios/) — Stating your role and the constraints you worked under. Purpose: Supports an evidence-led account of a career change. Free article, no account; the site sells courses and reports that are not required. Verified 2026-09-06. US hiring context; the framing advice transfers. Fallback: R23.
- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — The responsibilities the role actually involves. Purpose: Keeps the transfer claims tied to what the work requires rather than to enthusiasm. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer's description; other roles differ and this establishes nothing about the market. Fallback: R22.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**The account is true, including the unflattering parts**

Adequate evidence: A version you would be comfortable having checked.

0 — Shaped into destiny.

1 — True but sanded smooth.

2 — True including the awkward parts.

3 — As adequate, and it names a real dissatisfaction without blaming anyone.

Repair: Write what actually happened, then remove what you added to make it neat. Recheck: The account.

**Transfer and non-transfer are both named**

Adequate evidence: Two specific lists.

0 — Everything claimed as transferable.

1 — Transfer named, gaps omitted.

2 — Both named specifically.

3 — As adequate, and the gaps match your matrix.

Repair: Name three things your earlier work did not teach you. Recheck: The two lists.

**It closes on evidence rather than enthusiasm**

Adequate evidence: A final sentence naming what exists.

0 — Ends on passion.

1 — Mentions work vaguely.

2 — Names specific evidence.

3 — As adequate, and it invites a question about the work.

Repair: Replace the closing sentence with what you can show. Recheck: The closing.

**It fits ninety seconds when spoken**

Adequate evidence: Timed rehearsals within the limit.

0 — Untimed or well over.

1 — Close to time.

2 — Within ninety seconds.

3 — As adequate, and the specifics survived the cutting.

Repair: Cut transitions rather than specifics and time it again. Recheck: The timings.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson writes to people, one at a time.

**Review criteria:**

- The account is true, including the unflattering parts
- Transfer and non-transfer are both named
- It closes on evidence rather than enthusiasm
- It fits ninety seconds when spoken

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The true version is usually better than the constructed one. Something happened — work that looked good and did not help anyone, a project where the brief was wrong, a growing interest in why rather than how — and saying it plainly is more convincing than a story shaped to sound inevitable.
- Being specific about the transfer is what makes it credible. Graphic design gives typography, hierarchy, colour discipline, working to a brief and finishing things, and it does not give research methods, systems thinking or the practice of testing your own work. Saying both is what distinguishes a serious answer from a pitch.
- Ninety seconds, because it is an opener rather than the interview. Practise it to length; the version that runs four minutes crowds out the questions you actually want to be asked.
- Never disparage the old field. It reads as disloyalty and as a preview of how you will talk about this employer later, and it is also untrue: the skills are real and you are using them.
- End on what exists. “I have three projects, one with a measured before and after, and a portfolio with the limits written down” is a better closing sentence than any statement of passion, because it invites the next question toward your evidence. When that question comes, the count travels with its period, its owner and its limit — the owner's tally of eleven progress-chasing calls in five working days before the job-slip change, then seven in the five after, from one uncontrolled comparison — never as a rate, a per-day figure or a result of the demonstration page.

[NN/g: UX portfolio](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ux-design-portfolios/).

</details>

## Lesson 9: Writing to a person, one at a time

Stable ID: m20-l09-v1. Core.

Outreach that is automated, mass-sent or exaggerated damages the only reputation you have, and it does not work.

Bring: The vacancy captures and a published portfolio.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Individually written messages, each naming something specific
- A small, answerable ask in each
- A written rule against bulk sending and automation
- A follow-up rule with an interval and a limit

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: Recipients chosen for a reason, with what you read of theirs named precisely.

**Words you will use**

- **A specific person:** Somebody connected to work you have read. A general address gets a general message, which is the failure this lesson is about.
- **Reading first:** Their design notes, a talk, a listing’s wording. It is what makes the message possible; without it there is nothing specific to say.
- **From scratch:** Each one written for that person. A template with the name changed reads as a template, and people who receive many recognise it instantly.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Writing outreach, and writing one good message thirty times. Wrote a message I was pleased with, then sent it to thirty people with the name and company changed. It was honest, specific about my own work, and well written.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Each recipient by role and organisation, not their name or address, and why them”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Draft outreach messages that are specific, honest and individually written, and decide the rules under which you will send them.

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Six messages over two weeks, each written from scratch. One named a specific accessibility decision in the company's published design notes and asked a real question about it. One responded to a listing's line about designers running their own research and described the repair-shop project's five sessions in two sentences with a link. Each asked for twenty minutes or an answer to one question. Two replied, one with a conversation and one with a decline; one bounced because the address no longer existed; three did not reply and received one follow-up each after ten days, and one of those then replied. That is three replies from six messages, too few to say which wording worked. Nothing was automated, and no message was reused.

### Write individually

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

Each message written from scratch, two short paragraphs, naming something specific.

- Write each message from scratch, naming something specific.
- Keep it to two short paragraphs.

**Start here:** Write the first paragraph about them before writing anything about yourself.

**Enough:** Each message contains something that could only have been written to that person.

**From scratch:** Each one written for that person. A template with the name changed reads as a template, and people who receive many recognise it instantly.

**Two paragraphs:** What you noticed and what you made, then the ask. Longer is not read.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Writing outreach, and writing one good message thirty times.

**What I did:** Wrote a message I was pleased with, then sent it to thirty people with the name and company changed. It was honest, specific about my own work, and well written.

**What came back:** One reply out of thirty, and it asked which of their projects I meant.

**Why that question:** The paragraph about their work had to fit thirty companies, so it said things like the thoughtfulness of your product decisions. It named nothing, because it could not.

**What six messages produced:** Two replies, one bounced address and three silences. Of the two that replied, one named an accessibility decision in their published design notes and asked a real question about it; the other answered a listing’s line about designers running their own research.

**What I think the difference was:** Not effort per message. Whether there was anything in it that could only have been written to that person.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is scaling a good message, because the message is good and the only apparent cost is your time. Anything that must fit thirty recipients names nothing, and the recognisable generic paragraph is attached to your name permanently.

**Trade-off:** Six messages in two weeks feels far too slow when you want work, and thirty produced one confused reply.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how much of the difference is the reading and how much is luck. Two replies from six is a small sample and it is the one I have.

### Set the ask

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

One small, answerable ask per message, with anything too large removed.

- Ask for one small, answerable thing.
- Remove any request a stranger could not grant.

**Start here:** Write the ask as a question with a short answer.

**Enough:** Every ask could be granted in a few minutes.

**Small and answerable:** Twenty minutes, or one question they can answer in three sentences. Something a stranger can say yes to without arranging anything.

**Too large for a stranger:** A referral, a review of your portfolio, an introduction to their hiring manager. All ask them to spend their own credibility on somebody they have not met.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-1.

Six made up asks from outreach messages. For each one, decide what it is.

“Could I ask one question: does your team expect designers to recruit their own participants, or does research handle it?”

- small and answerable
- too large for a stranger
- not really an ask

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

small and answerable — Three sentences to answer, specific, and the answer is useful whatever it is.

too large for a stranger — It costs a minute.

not really an ask — It is a clear question with a clear answer.

Now read your own asks and decide which group each falls into.

</details>

### Write your rules

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

A written rule against automation and bulk sending, and a follow-up rule with an interval and a limit.

- Write your rule against automation and bulk sending.
- Write the follow-up interval and the limit of one.

**Start here:** Write both rules before sending the first message.

**Enough:** The rules are specific enough to break knowingly rather than by drift.

**No automation:** No sequencing tool, no mail merge, no generated batch. Sent by you, one at a time, from your own address.

**The follow-up rule:** One follow-up, after about ten days. Silence is an answer, and a second follow-up converts a non-reply into a bad impression.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-demo.

Made-up example. Following up on silence, and following up until there is an answer.

**What I did:** Three follow-ups to one person over a fortnight: a polite nudge, then a question in case the first had not arrived, then a short note saying I would stop bothering them.

**What I told myself:** That people are busy and messages get buried, both of which are true, and that persistence is usually advised.

**What the third message did:** Made my name memorable in the wrong way, in a field where people talk to each other. The fourth message would have been about me rather than to them.

**What the rule is now:** One follow-up, after ten days, adding something rather than repeating. Then silence is the answer and the record says no reply.

**What that produced:** Of three non-replies, one answered the single follow-up. The other two stayed silent, and there is nothing attached to my name.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is following up until something happens, because persistence is widely advised and silence feels unfinished. A non-reply costs nothing; a third message converts it into a bad impression in a small field.

**Trade-off:** Accepting silence means never knowing whether the message arrived, and occasionally it genuinely did not.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether ten days is the right interval. It is long enough that the follow-up is not a nudge and short enough to still be in context.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Writing to a person, one at a time
What I am trying to do: Draft outreach messages that are specific, honest and individually written, and decide the rules under which you will send them.

Key idea or terms:
A specific person: Somebody connected to work you have read. A general address gets a general message, which is the failure this lesson is about.
Reading first: Their design notes, a talk, a listing’s wording. It is what makes the message possible; without it there is nothing specific to say.
From scratch: Each one written for that person. A template with the name changed reads as a template, and people who receive many recognise it instantly.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Writing outreach, and writing one good message thirty times. Wrote a message I was pleased with, then sent it to thirty people with the name and company changed. It was honest, specific about my own work, and well written.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Each recipient by role and organisation, not their name or address, and why them” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — How the employer describes its process and what it values. Purpose: Shows the kind of specific detail worth naming in a message. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer; use it as an example of reading before writing, not as a template for what to say. Fallback: R22.
- R54: [GOV.UK: managing research data and participant privacy](https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/user-research/managing-user-research-data-participant-privacy) — Handling information about identifiable people. Purpose: Keeps participant and client details out of messages to strangers. Free reading, no account; published 5 November 2018. Verified 2026-09-06. UK GDPR framing; the discipline about other people's data is what applies here. Fallback: R53.

<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**Each message is individually written and specific**

Adequate evidence: Messages naming something particular to the recipient.

0 — A template with names substituted.

1 — Partly personalised.

2 — Individually written throughout.

3 — As adequate, and each names something you actually read.

Repair: Rewrite each message from scratch after reading something of theirs. Recheck: The messages.

**Each ask is small and answerable**

Adequate evidence: One specific request per message.

0 — Asks for a referral or a job.

1 — Vague ask.

2 — One small, answerable ask.

3 — As adequate, and the ask is proportionate to a stranger's time.

Repair: Replace the ask with one question or twenty minutes. Recheck: The asks.

**Automation and bulk sending are excluded by rule**

Adequate evidence: A written rule, and any messages sent by hand.

0 — Bulk or automated sending used.

1 — Manual but templated.

2 — Written rule, and any sending done by hand.

3 — As adequate, and no outreach tool or contact database was used.

Repair: Stop any automated sending, write the rule, and send by hand. Recheck: The rule and the sent messages.

**A follow-up rule with an interval and a limit exists**

Adequate evidence: A written interval and a maximum of one follow-up.

0 — No rule, or repeated chasing.

1 — Interval without a limit.

2 — Interval and limit written.

3 — As adequate, and silence is recorded as normal rather than as failure.

Repair: Write the interval and the limit before sending anything. Recheck: The rule.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson prepares for the conversations these produce.

**Review criteria:**

- Each message is individually written and specific
- Each ask is small and answerable
- Automation and bulk sending are excluded by rule
- A follow-up rule with an interval and a limit exists

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- Specificity is the whole method. A message that names something particular — a decision in their published work, a problem their listing described, a page you actually read — is more likely to be answered than a generic one, and writing it takes fifteen minutes rather than three.
- The structure is short: what you noticed, what you have made that is relevant, and what you are asking for. Two paragraphs. Anything longer is asking a stranger for time before you have given them a reason.
- Bulk and automation are excluded here as a matter of practice, not of squeamishness. Mass messages are recognisable, they are frequently a nuisance to the recipient, and they associate your name with that behaviour permanently. This course does not send anything automatically and neither should you.
- Ask small. A specific question about their work, or twenty minutes of conversation, is answerable. “Can you refer me” is not, from a stranger, and asking it converts a possible relationship into a closed one.
- Silence is the normal outcome and is not information about your worth. One follow-up after a reasonable interval is proportionate; more than that is pressure, and it is remembered.

[GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/).

</details>

## Lesson 10: Interview practice: the walkthrough and the examples

Stable ID: m20-l10-v1. Core.

The conversation, not the portfolio, is where most decisions are actually made.

Bring: The ten-minute walkthrough and prepared question answers.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A practised walkthrough delivered under interruption
- Three real behavioural examples in situation-action-result form
- One example where you were wrong
- A recording and a note of two weaknesses
- One example revised against a requirement from a vacancy you captured

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: Three real instances in situation-action-result form, each able to survive three follow-ups, and one revised against a requirement from your captures.

**Words you will use**

- **An instance:** One occasion, with a date and a specific thing that happened. I usually try to is a policy, and it is what people give when they have not prepared.
- **Three questions deep:** The depth a real example survives and a constructed one does not. Detail runs out on the second question and the answer starts generalising.
- **Where you were wrong:** A real error with no rescue in it. Its believability is exactly what the other two examples borrow.

**Quick example.** A made-up interruption during a walkthrough of the practice repair-shop project. The interviewer asks: “So what changed after your work?” Your notes: before the project the owner estimated six to ten progress calls a day but had never counted. After the shop changed how it wrote job slips, his tally showed eleven calls over the five working days before the change and seven over the five after. The status page was a demonstration that no customer used.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Three instances, each with the situation, what you did and what happened”.

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. A ninety-minute practice with someone who had not seen the work. Ten-minute walkthrough of project three, interrupted four times, twice on the measurement and twice on the sample. Asked what changed, the answer gave the owner's tally — eleven progress-chasing calls in five working days before the shop changed its job slips, seven in the five after — said that one uncontrolled comparison could not separate the change from an ordinary quieter week, and said the status page was only a demonstration. Three behavioural examples prepared: a disagreement with a reviewer that was resolved by testing rather than argument; a failed repair with what it changed; and a recruitment failure with what was done instead. The interviewer pushed three questions deep on the second, which held because it was real. Two weaknesses noted afterwards: drifting into the interface when asked about a decision, and answering a question about limits by defending the work before conceding.

### Keep this practice material beside you

Section: learn. Stable action: supplied-material.

Use your own material, or the labelled practice material below. Keep its source labels attached; practice material is never evidence about real people.

- Made-up evidence record from the course’s practice repair-shop project, used in this lesson’s examples. It is not your data; prepare your answers from your own records.
- Before the project, the owner estimated six to ten progress calls a day; he had never counted them. After the shop changed how it wrote job slips, his tally showed eleven progress-chasing calls over the five working days before the change and seven over the five after: one uncontrolled comparison.
- The status page was only a demonstration; no customer used it. The learner did the research and built the demonstration alone; the owner changed the job slips and kept the tally.
- Solo interruption slips, made up: “Why that and not something else?” “How many people was that?” “Who counted?” “What would you do differently?” “What does that number not show?” “Was that you or the owner?” “What did you leave out?” “How do you know?”


### Prepare the examples

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

Three real instances in situation-action-result form, each able to survive three follow-ups, and one revised against a requirement from your captures.

- Write three real instances with situation, action and result.
- Check each could survive three follow-up questions.
- Revise one so it answers a requirement from a vacancy you captured, keeping its counts, its owner and its limits.

**Start here:** Write the occasion with its date before writing what it shows.

**Enough:** Each example is one occasion rather than a habit.

**An instance:** One occasion, with a date and a specific thing that happened. I usually try to is a policy, and it is what people give when they have not prepared.

**Three questions deep:** The depth a real example survives and a constructed one does not. Detail runs out on the second question and the answer starts generalising.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Preparing behavioural examples, and preparing answers about how I work.

**What I prepared:** Good answers about how I handle disagreement, how I deal with failure and how I work with constraints. Thought through, honest, and true of me generally.

**What the first follow-up was:** Can you give me an example of that. The thing I had not prepared, asked immediately, on all three.

**What came out:** A half-remembered situation assembled while speaking. It survived one question and lost its detail on the second, which is exactly what a fabricated example does.

**What I prepared instead:** Three occasions. A reviewer who disagreed about the status wording, settled by testing it with two people rather than by arguing. A repair that failed and what it changed. A recruitment attempt that produced nobody, and what I did instead.

**What happened in the practice:** The interviewer went three questions deep on the failed repair and it held, because everything asked for was in the records.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is preparing your approach rather than occasions, because the questions are phrased generally and a general answer sounds responsive. The follow-up is always for an example, and one assembled under pressure collapses at the second question.

**Trade-off:** Three specific occasions cover less ground than three general answers, and one of mine did not fit a question I was asked.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether three is enough. Two of the three were used in the practice, and the third has never come up.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-1.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“A reviewer disagreed with my status wording. Rather than argue, I tested both versions with two people, and both read the original as a promise, so I changed it.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — One occasion, a specific disagreement, and a resolution somebody can ask three questions about.

a policy, not an instance — It describes one occasion rather than a habit.

would collapse under follow-up — The sessions and the change are in the records.

Now read your own three and check that each is one occasion rather than a habit.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-2.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“I usually try to find the shared goal and work back from there.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — Nothing happened in it.

a policy, not an instance — A very common answer to a disagreement question, and the follow-up is usually can you give me an example.

would collapse under follow-up — It has nothing to collapse; the example that follows might.

Now read your own three and check that each is one occasion rather than a habit.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 3 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-3.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“At my agency I once convinced a large client to abandon a redesign by presenting user research.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — It is shaped like one and it is doing too much: a large client, an abandoned redesign, research that decided it.

a policy, not an instance — It is specific, which is what makes it risky.

would collapse under follow-up — Which client, what research, how many people, what did they say. An answer built to impress runs out of detail at the second question.

Now read your own three and check that each is one occasion rather than a habit.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-4.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“My first repair to the wording made it worse: the next person read the window as a guarantee. I changed it again and re-tested, and that is why I now re-test every repair.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — A failure, what it changed, and a habit that came from it. This is the one interviewers push on, and it holds.

a policy, not an instance — The habit is at the end, where it belongs, after the occasion.

would collapse under follow-up — Every detail is in the session notes.

Now read your own three and check that each is one occasion rather than a habit.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 5 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-5.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“I always make sure accessibility is considered from the start.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — No occasion is described.

a policy, not an instance — Your own matrix probably says the screen-reader pass was skipped in two projects, which makes always a risky word.

would collapse under follow-up — It would, and the first problem is that it is not an example.

### Try the distinction · 6 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-sort-6.

Six made up answers to behavioural interview questions. For each one, decide what it is.

“I tried to recruit people who had stopped using the shop and got nobody, so I said so in the limits and wrote what I would do differently.”

- a real instance
- a policy, not an instance
- would collapse under follow-up

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a real instance — A failure with no rescue in it, which is why it is believable and why it is worth preparing.

a policy, not an instance — It happened once, on a date.

would collapse under follow-up — It survives because there is nothing in it to defend.

Now read your own three and check that each is one occasion rather than a habit.

### Choose the wrong one

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

An example where you were wrong, ending on what you did differently.

- Prepare an example where you were wrong and what changed.
- End it on what you did differently afterwards.

**Start here:** Choose one where you were plainly wrong rather than unlucky.

**Enough:** The example ends on what changed rather than on the mistake.

**Where you were wrong:** A real error with no rescue in it. Its believability is exactly what the other two examples borrow.

**Ending on the change:** What you did differently afterwards. Without it the example is a confession; with it, it is the calibration everybody is testing for.
