### Run the practice

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

A practice walkthrough of one project under interruption — a partner’s, or timed slips on the solo route — recorded.

- Ask someone to interrupt and to push follow-ups; with nobody available, use timed interruption slips and label it a solo rehearsal.
- Record it.

**Start here:** Ask your partner to interrupt at least four times, whether or not it feels natural. Solo: write eight interruption questions on slips and set a timer to ring at random.

**Enough:** You were stopped mid-section and picked the thread back up.

**One project:** Walked through properly. Three badly is the alternative, and range is already visible in the portfolio.

**Interruption:** What actually happens. A walkthrough rehearsed uninterrupted falls apart the first time somebody stops you at minute three.


### Try a supplied example

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-try.

A made-up interruption during a walkthrough of the practice repair-shop project. The interviewer asks: “So what changed after your work?” Your notes: before the project the owner estimated six to ten progress calls a day but had never counted. After the shop changed how it wrote job slips, his tally showed eleven calls over the five working days before the change and seven over the five after. The status page was a demonstration that no customer used.

Which answer keeps the units, the ownership and the uncertainty?

- Calls dropped from eleven a day to seven a day across two matched working weeks after the job-slip change.
- After the shop changed its job slips, his tally went from eleven calls in five working days to seven in the next five.
- We cut his progress calls by more than a third with the status page, and he said the week felt calmer.
- Calls fell from six to ten a day to seven, so the change roughly halved his interruptions within a week.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Calls dropped from eleven a day to seven a day across two matched working weeks after the job-slip change. — The tally was eleven over five working days, not eleven a day; a day multiplies both counts by five. Dropped after the change also slides towards a cause the comparison cannot show.

After the shop changed its job slips, his tally went from eleven calls in five working days to seven in the next five. — The owner kept the tally, each count keeps its five-day period, and the change is named without claiming it caused the fall. Say next that one comparison like this cannot rule out an ordinary quieter week; conceding first moves the conversation on.

We cut his progress calls by more than a third with the status page, and he said the week felt calmer. — We claims a team, cut claims the cause, more than a third turns four calls into a rate, and the status page was a demonstration nobody used. His feeling is worth quoting as his, not as a result.

Calls fell from six to ten a day to seven, so the change roughly halved his interruptions within a week. — Six to ten a day was his uncounted estimate; seven is a count over five working days. One sentence comparing them mixes a guess per day with a tally per period, and halved is arithmetic on neither.

Now say your own measurement answer aloud with each count’s period, who counted, and the condition you cannot separate.

</details>

### Review

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

Two specific weaknesses from the recording, and whether you conceded before defending.

- Watch once and note two specific weaknesses.
- Check whether you conceded before defending.

**Start here:** Watch it once with a notebook rather than asking how it went.

**Enough:** Both weaknesses are things somebody could act on.

**A specific weakness:** Drifting into the interface when asked about a decision. Not I was nervous.

**Conceding first:** The habit from Module 19, tested here under interruption. Under pressure the prepared order is the first thing to go.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-demo.

Made-up example. Reviewing a practice interview, and asking how it went.

**What I asked my partner:** How did that go. She said it went well and that I clearly knew the work, which was kind and told me nothing.

**What the recording showed:** Twice, asked about a decision, I answered by describing the interface. Both times the question was why and both times I said what.

**The second thing:** On the question about the small sample, I gave the periods and the matching before conceding anything, which is the order Module 19 warned about.

**Why neither came up in conversation:** Both are invisible to a listener who is following along. They are only audible on a recording, played back, listening for the order of the sentences.

**What I did with them:** Redid the decision section with why first, and the limits answer with the concession first. Fifteen minutes, both recordings kept.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is reviewing by asking your partner how it went, because they are polite and they were following the content rather than the shape of your answers. The recording is what catches a why answered with a what.

**Trade-off:** Watching yourself in a practice interview is unpleasant enough that most people do it once and call it done.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether the drift happens when the question is friendly. Both instances here were gentle questions, which is mildly worrying.

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

An interviewer says “walk me through your work”. Should you cover all three projects to show range?

- All three, since walk me through your work usually means the whole portfolio, not one piece.
- All three briefly to establish breadth, then go deeper on whichever one they react to.
- One project, walked through properly; range is already visible in the portfolio itself.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

All three, since walk me through your work usually means the whole portfolio, not one piece. — Sometimes it does, and asking which they want is better than guessing; unasked, one project properly.

All three briefly to establish breadth, then go deeper on whichever one they react to. — Brief walkthroughs of three projects is the shape that leaves no time for the follow-ups.

One project, walked through properly; range is already visible in the portfolio itself. — One project under interruption, four times, twice on the measurement and twice on the sample, is what a walkthrough actually has to survive. Show more if they ask.

Improve: Run the practice on one project, with at least four interruptions, in step 3. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: You were stopped mid-section and picked the thread back up.

Answers to revisit: interruptions, recording-where, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

You have prepared good answers about how you handle disagreement. Is that enough?

- Prepared approaches plus one example loosely in mind are enough, ready in case they ask for one.
- Prepared approaches are enough, because behavioural questions are phrased generally and answered that way.
- Prepared occasions are needed too: the follow-up usually asks for an example, and improvised ones thin out.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Prepared approaches plus one example loosely in mind are enough, ready in case they ask for one. — Loosely in mind is what loses its detail at the second question; ready means written and checked against three follow-ups.

Prepared approaches are enough, because behavioural questions are phrased generally and answered that way. — They are phrased generally and answered specifically, which is what the follow-up is for.

Prepared occasions are needed too: the follow-up usually asks for an example, and improvised ones thin out. — Three occasions with dates — a disagreement settled by testing, a failed repair, a recruitment that produced nobody — survive three questions because everything asked for is in the records.

Improve: Write three occasions with dates in step 1 and the three follow-ups each must survive. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Each example is one occasion rather than a habit.

Answers to revisit: three-examples, survives-followups, example-revised, improvement-made

</details>

Their view is a useful signal once combined with your own sense of how the practice went. — Both are impressions of the content; the recording catches the order of your sentences.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: In a practice interview for a library-systems role, Leela is asked, “Tell me about a time you improved a process.” She answers: “We completely transformed how returns were handled and cut waiting times by half.” Her notes: she alone redesigned the returns-shelf labels; the desk supervisor timed queues at about 12 minutes on average across three Saturdays before and about 6 across three Saturdays after; a second staff member started on Saturdays in the same month.

**Task:** Rewrite her answer so it would survive three follow-up questions, and explain what you changed and why.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps “we”, “transformed” or “cut by half”, or removes the numbers rather than stating them with their period.
- Adequate: Says she redesigned the labels; gives the supervisor’s timings (about 12 then about 6 minutes, three Saturdays each); does not claim the labels caused the fall.
- Strong: Adequate, plus concedes the second staff member as a competing explanation before defending, and prepares the likely follow-ups: who timed it, over how many Saturdays.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

Optional: may be left empty.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Interview practice: the walkthrough and the examples
What I am trying to do: Run a full practice interview covering the portfolio walkthrough and three behavioural examples, recorded and reviewed.

Key idea or terms:
An instance: One occasion, with a date and a specific thing that happened. I usually try to is a policy, and it is what people give when they have not prepared.
Three questions deep: The depth a real example survives and a constructed one does not. Detail runs out on the second question and the answer starts generalising.
Where you were wrong: A real error with no rescue in it. Its believability is exactly what the other two examples borrow.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
A made-up interruption during a walkthrough of the practice repair-shop project. The interviewer asks: “So what changed after your work?” Your notes: before the project the owner estimated six to ten progress calls a day but had never counted. After the shop changed how it wrote job slips, his tally showed eleven calls over the five working days before the change and seven over the five after. The status page was a demonstration that no customer used.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Three instances, each with the situation, what you did and what happened” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “Three instances, each with the situation, what you did and what happened”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

**Continue without AI:** Stay in this course and use the first “Try a supplied example” question. Choose an answer, read the explanation, then return to “Three instances, each with the situation, what you did and what happened” and write one sentence in your own words.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Ask your practice partner to interrupt at least four times.
- Replace any general answer with a specific instance from your records.

- R22: [NN/g: UX portfolio](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ux-design-portfolios/) — Talking through your process using process artefacts. Purpose: Supports the walkthrough half of the practice. Free article, no account; the site sells courses and reports that are not required. Verified 2026-09-06. US hiring context. Fallback: R23.
- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — The stages of the hiring process and what each examines. Purpose: Shapes what the practice interview should cover. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer's process; others differ substantially, and this is not evidence about hiring in India. Fallback: R22.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**One project walked through under interruption**

Adequate evidence: A practice walkthrough with interruptions handled.

0 — Uninterrupted monologue, or several projects.

1 — One project, few interruptions.

2 — One project, interrupted and handled — by a partner, or by timed slips on the labelled solo route.

3 — As adequate, and the answers stayed brief under pressure.

Repair: Ask your practice partner to interrupt at least four times. Recheck: The recording.

**Three real examples in situation-action-result form**

Adequate evidence: Three specific instances, each defensible three questions deep.

0 — General statements of approach.

1 — Specific but shallow.

2 — Three instances that hold under follow-up.

3 — As adequate, and each names what actually happened afterwards.

Repair: Replace any general answer with a specific instance from your records. Recheck: The examples.

**An example where you were wrong, with the change**

Adequate evidence: A real error and what you did differently after it.

0 — None, or a disguised strength.

1 — An error without a change.

2 — An error with a real change.

3 — As adequate, and the change is visible in a later project.

Repair: Take the failed repair or recruitment failure and prepare it. Recheck: The example.

**A recording reviewed with two weaknesses named**

Adequate evidence: A recording and two specific, actionable weaknesses.

0 — No recording.

1 — Recorded, not reviewed.

2 — Reviewed with two weaknesses.

3 — As adequate, and one section was redone with the correction.

Repair: Watch the recording and write two specific weaknesses. Recheck: The review notes.

**Figures in the story keep their period, owner and limits**

Adequate evidence: The measurement answer as said in practice: counts with their periods, who counted, and the condition that limits them.

0 — A rate, a per-day figure made from a period count, or a causal verb.

1 — Counts given, with the period or the owner missing.

2 — Counts with their periods and owner, and the limiting condition conceded.

3 — As adequate, and any estimate is kept apart from the count it might be confused with.

Repair: Rewrite the measurement answer from your records and say it aloud again. Recheck: The rewritten answer.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson covers exercises, take-homes and when to say no.

**Review criteria:**

- One project walked through under interruption
- Three real examples in situation-action-result form
- An example where you were wrong, with the change
- A recording reviewed with two weaknesses named
- Figures in the story keep their period, owner and limits

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- One project done properly is the right answer to “walk me through your work”. The m19 ten-minute version is exactly this, and the discipline is not to drift into the other two when the first one gets a good reaction.
- Behavioural questions want a specific instance, and the reliable shape is the situation, what you did, and what happened. Vague answers about how you generally approach things are the most common failure and the easiest to fix: prepare three real instances and use them.
- The follow-up is what tests truth. An invented example survives one question and fails the second, because the details do not exist to be asked about. Every example you prepare should be one you could answer three questions deep.
- Include one where you were wrong, because it will be asked and because it is the strongest example you have. The failed repair from m18, the finding you misread, the participant you should have recruited — each has a real ending and shows the calibration the whole interview is testing.
- Practise with someone who interrupts, since real interviews do. The prepared paragraph delivered uninterrupted is not the skill; answering the question actually asked, briefly, is. If nobody is available, write eight interruption questions on slips, set a timer to ring at random, and answer the top slip when it rings; label it a solo rehearsal, because it practises recovering from interruption, not reading a real interviewer.
- Numbers get the hardest follow-ups, so prepare them from your records rather than from memory. Keep each count with its period and its owner, keep an estimate apart from a count, and concede the condition you cannot separate before defending the rest. “After the shop changed its job slips, his tally went from eleven calls over five working days to seven over the next five, and one comparison like that can't rule out an ordinary quiet week” survives three questions; “my page cut calls from eleven a day to seven” does not survive the first.
- Revise against a real listing rather than a model answer. Take one requirement from a vacancy you captured and rework one of your own examples so it answers that requirement, keeping its counts, its owner and its limits. If none of your examples can answer it, that is the honest finding, and it belongs in your matrix rather than in an improvised story.

[NN/g: UX portfolio](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ux-design-portfolios/).

</details>

## Lesson 11: Exercises, take-homes and saying no

Stable ID: m20-l11-v1. Core.

Take-home tasks are where unpaid work is most often extracted, and where scope judgement is most visible.

Bring: Your m17 and m18 scoping work.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A take-home protocol: fixed time, scope cut, cuts stated
- A question to ask before starting any exercise
- A written decline for briefs that are real product work
- Two practised live exercises focused on reasoning aloud

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: A fixed-time rule with scope as the variable, and the sentence stating the cuts.

**Words you will use**

- **Fixed time, variable scope:** Module 14’s appetite, applied to yourself. Four hours means four hours, and what fits is a decision you make and explain.
- **Stating the cuts:** The part that is actually being assessed. It shows scope judgement, which is a core part of the job and invisible in a finished artefact.
- **What are you assessing:** The most useful question there is, and asking it is itself assessed. The answer changes what you build.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Doing a four-hour take-home, and doing more than asked. Eleven hours on a four-hour exercise. Both flows, full visual design, a short deck, and a second concept in case they preferred it.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Your rule: the time is fixed and the scope moves”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Prepare for design exercises and take-home tasks, including how to negotiate scope and when to decline.


### Treat a take-home as a scoping problem: fixed time, variable scope

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

Treat a take-home as a scoping problem: fixed time, variable scope.


### Ask what they are assessing before you start

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-2.

Ask what they are assessing before you start.


### State the time you spent and what you cut

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-3.

State the time you spent and what you cut.


### Decline tasks that are real work for their product

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Decline tasks that are real work for their product.


### In a live exercise, think aloud and ask about constraints

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-5.

In a live exercise, think aloud and ask about constraints.


### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. Two exercises. The first, a four-hour take-home about an onboarding flow, was fixed at four hours: structure, main flow and five error states delivered, visual design and a second flow cut, with a note saying so and why. The second brief asked for three production-ready screens redesigning a live page of the company's product, and was declined in two sentences — the work was theirs to be paid for — with an offer to do a two-hour neutral exercise or to walk through an existing project instead. That offer was accepted. A live exercise was practised twice with someone playing the interviewer, focusing on asking about constraints before drawing anything.


### Choose where you will do the work

Section: learn. Stable action: workspace.

Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Keep this practice material beside you

Section: learn. Stable action: supplied-material.

Use your own material, or the labelled practice material below. Keep its source labels attached; practice material is never evidence about real people.

- Made-up live-exercise problems for the solo route. Set forty minutes, write three questions before drawing, answer each only from the stated facts or mark it unknown, and think aloud into a recording. Label it solo rehearsal.
- Problem A: a small lending library wants members to know when a reserved book is ready. Stated facts: members are adults; most reserve online; staff update the system once a day.
- Problem B: a community sports hall wants fewer no-shows for booked badminton courts. Stated facts: bookings come by phone and online; a no-show leaves a court empty for an hour.
- Problem C: a weekly farmers’ market wants shoppers to know which stalls are open before they travel. Stated facts: stallholders decide on the morning; many shoppers come by bus.


### Write the protocol

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

A fixed-time rule with scope as the variable, and the sentence stating the cuts.

- Write your fixed-time rule and how you will cut scope.
- Include how you will state the time and the cuts.

**Start here:** Write the time down before reading the brief properly.

**Enough:** Your rule says what moves when the time runs out.

**Fixed time, variable scope:** Module 14’s appetite, applied to yourself. Four hours means four hours, and what fits is a decision you make and explain.

**Stating the cuts:** The part that is actually being assessed. It shows scope judgement, which is a core part of the job and invisible in a finished artefact.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Doing a four-hour take-home, and doing more than asked.

**What I did:** Eleven hours on a four-hour exercise. Both flows, full visual design, a short deck, and a second concept in case they preferred it.

**Why I did it:** It was the strongest submission I could make, and eleven hours of my own time felt like a cheap way to stand out.

**What the feedback said:** That the work was good and they could not tell how I would handle a real deadline, because nothing in it had been cut.

**What the four-hour version would have shown:** Structure, main flow, five error states, and a note saying visual design and the second flow were cut because the error states show more about how I think.

**What it also did to everybody else:** A candidate who fits the time is competing against submissions like mine. Overrunning is quietly unfair as well as uninformative.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is doing more than asked, because it looks like enthusiasm and the extra time is yours to spend. It shows you cannot scope, which is precisely what a timed exercise exists to test.

**Trade-off:** A four-hour submission beside an eleven-hour one looks thinner, and some reviewers will read it that way despite the note.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how many reviewers read the note at all. It is the first line of my submission now, rather than the last.

### Prepare the questions

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

Questions prepared for before you start, including what is being assessed and who the users are.

- Write what you will ask before starting a take-home.
- Include what is being assessed and who the users are.

**Start here:** Send the questions before starting the clock, not alongside the submission.

**Enough:** You have three questions you will ask every time.

**What are you assessing:** The most useful question there is, and asking it is itself assessed. The answer changes what you build.

**Who the users are:** Often unanswerable, which is its own finding and something to state in the submission.

### Draft the decline

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

A short decline for a brief that is real product work, with an alternative offered.

- Write a short, polite decline for a brief that is real product work.
- Include an alternative you would happily do.

**Start here:** Write the decline now, calmly, so it exists before you need it.

**Enough:** The decline is two sentences and offers something instead.

**Real product work:** Production-ready screens for a live page of their product. The output is usable by them, which makes it work rather than assessment.

**Declining well:** Two sentences, no accusation, and an alternative you would happily do. Most briefs that cross the line are thoughtless rather than exploitative.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-1.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

A four-hour take-home on onboarding for a fictional product, with the time stated and a note that partial submissions are expected.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — Bounded, fictional, and explicit that not everything need be finished. This is what a good brief looks like.

negotiate the scope first — The scope is yours to cut already, which is the point of the exercise.

decline — it is real product work — Nothing they receive is usable in their product.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-2.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

Three production-ready screens redesigning a live page of their own product.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — The output is usable by them on a page that exists.

negotiate the scope first — Cutting the scope does not change what the work is for.

decline — it is real product work — Two sentences, no accusation, and an alternative: a neutral exercise, or a walkthrough of an existing project.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 3 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-3.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

A take-home with no time stated and a brief that would take about twenty hours to do fully.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — An unstated time means the candidate with most free time submits most.

negotiate the scope first — Propose four hours and say what you will deliver in them. Most briefs like this are thoughtless and the answer is usually yes.

decline — it is real product work — The problem is unbounded scope rather than the work being theirs.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-4.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

A forty-minute live exercise on a problem you are given in the session.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — Short, bounded, and it assesses reasoning aloud rather than output. Ask about constraints before drawing.

negotiate the scope first — Forty minutes is already the scope.

decline — it is real product work — Nothing produced in forty minutes is usable by anybody.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 5 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-5.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

A brief asking for a full product strategy and roadmap for their next quarter.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — A quarter’s roadmap is a piece of work they would otherwise pay for.

negotiate the scope first — A smaller version of their planning is still their planning.

decline — it is real product work — Offer to walk through how you approached scope on a project of your own instead.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 6 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-6.

Six made up exercise briefs. For each one, decide what you would do.

A take-home that requires a design tool you do not have a paid licence for.

- a fair exercise
- negotiate the scope first
- decline — it is real product work

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a fair exercise — A brief that requires a purchase is not fair to everybody who receives it.

negotiate the scope first — Say so and propose an alternative format. It is a reasonable message and the answer is usually fine.

decline — it is real product work — The work itself may be perfectly legitimate.

Now write your own two-sentence decline and the alternative you would offer.

</details>

### Practise a live exercise

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

A live exercise practised, with constraints asked before anything was drawn.

- Have someone give you a problem and forty minutes; solo, take a supplied made-up problem, record yourself and label it a rehearsal.
- Ask about constraints first and think aloud throughout.

**Start here:** Ask three questions before drawing anything at all.

**Enough:** Your reasoning is audible — to a partner, or on your own recording — rather than silent drawing.

**Thinking aloud:** What is being assessed in a live exercise. Silence followed by a good sketch tells them almost nothing.

**Asking first:** Who is this for, what do they have now, what is fixed. Three questions before a line is drawn.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-demo.

Made-up example. Doing a live exercise, and starting with the drawing.

**What I did:** Heard the problem — a way for people to track a repair — and started sketching within ninety seconds, because forty minutes is short and silence felt wasteful.

**What I produced:** Six screens of a tracking flow, neatly drawn, for a problem I had assumed rather than asked about.

**What I had not asked:** Who it was for, what they had already, and what was fixed. Three questions, about two minutes.

**What the answer to the second would have been:** That there was already a system the staff used, and the exercise was about the customer side of it. Half my screens were redesigning something not in scope.

**What the second practice looked like:** Three questions, four minutes, then thirty-five minutes of sketching with the constraints known and every choice said aloud as it was made.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is drawing early because the clock is running, when what is being assessed is the reasoning rather than the output. Silence followed by a good sketch tells them almost nothing, and a sketch from an unexamined brief answers a question nobody asked.

**Trade-off:** Four minutes of questions out of forty feels like a lot when you can already picture a solution.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how many interviewers mind the questions. My practice partner found two of the three obvious and the third useful.

- It shows commitment, as long as you leave out how long the submission actually took.
- It mainly shows you did not scope, which is what a timed exercise exists to test.

### Review

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-brief.

Where you started drawing before asking, with one section repeated.

- Note where you started drawing before asking.
- Repeat one section with the correction.

**Start here:** Ask your partner, or check your recording, for the minute you first drew something.

**Enough:** You know where the drawing started and what you had not asked yet.

**Drawing too early:** The usual failure, and it comes from wanting to look productive. The recording or your partner will tell you when it happened.

**Repeating a section:** Ten minutes with the correction, rather than the whole exercise again.

**Repair:** The one change a Check question asks you to make. Make it in the step it belongs to, then record here that you made it.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: A bakery chain asks a freelance illustrator to “show what she can do” by designing its new seasonal menu board — final, print-ready artwork for its twelve shops — with no fee and a one-week deadline. The best entry, it says, wins a year’s contract.

**Task:** Decide whether she should do it, negotiate it or decline it, and explain why, including what she could offer instead.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Does the full menu board to win the contract, or refuses rudely without offering anything.
- Adequate: Declines because print-ready artwork for their live shops is real work they would use; offers a short neutral exercise or a walkthrough of past work; two sentences, no accusation.
- Strong: Adequate, plus notes a paid trial would also be fair, asks what they are assessing, and observes that unpaid, winner-takes-all terms extract the most work from the most candidates.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

Optional: may be left empty.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.


### Review and finish your practice

Section: practice. Stable action: review-work.

Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The last lesson turns all of this into a practice you can hold for months.


<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Exercises, take-homes and saying no
What I am trying to do: Prepare for design exercises and take-home tasks, including how to negotiate scope and when to decline.

Key idea or terms:
Fixed time, variable scope: Module 14’s appetite, applied to yourself. Four hours means four hours, and what fits is a decision you make and explain.
Stating the cuts: The part that is actually being assessed. It shows scope judgement, which is a core part of the job and invisible in a finished artefact.
What are you assessing: The most useful question there is, and asking it is itself assessed. The answer changes what you build.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Doing a four-hour take-home, and doing more than asked. Eleven hours on a four-hour exercise. Both flows, full visual design, a short deck, and a second concept in case they preferred it.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Your rule: the time is fixed and the scope moves” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “Your rule: the time is fixed and the scope moves”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

**Continue without AI:** Stay in this course and use the first “Try the distinction” question. Choose an answer, read the explanation, then return to “Your rule: the time is fixed and the scope moves” and write one sentence in your own words.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Write the rule and the sentence you will include with any submission.
- Write the three questions you will always ask.

- R47: [Ryan Singer: Shape Up](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/webbook) — Setting the appetite, and fixed time with variable scope. Purpose: Supplies the reasoning for fixing the time and cutting the scope of a take-home. Free to read online; a print edition is sold and is not required. Verified 2026-09-06. One company's internal process; take the appetite reasoning, not the ceremony. Fallback: R07.
- R23: [GitLab: Product Designer role](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-description-library/product/product-designer/) — The exercise and interview stages the employer describes. Purpose: Shows what kind of exercise an employer may set and why. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer's process; the presence of an exercise there says nothing about prevalence elsewhere. Fallback: R22.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**A fixed-time protocol with stated cuts exists**

Adequate evidence: A written rule fixing time and cutting scope.

0 — No rule; time expands.

1 — Time fixed, cuts not declared.

2 — Time fixed and cuts declared.

3 — As adequate, and the declaration explains why those cuts.

Repair: Write the rule and the sentence you will include with any submission. Recheck: The protocol.

**Questions are prepared to ask before starting**

Adequate evidence: A short list including what is being assessed.

0 — None.

1 — General questions.

2 — Specific, including the assessment focus.

3 — As adequate, and it includes who the users are and any constraints.

Repair: Write the three questions you will always ask. Recheck: The question list.

**A decline is drafted for real product work**

Adequate evidence: A polite two-sentence decline with an alternative.

0 — No position; would do anything asked.

1 — Would decline without a plan.

2 — Drafted, polite, with an alternative.

3 — As adequate, and it names the boundary without accusation.

Repair: Write the message you would send when a brief crosses the line. Recheck: The draft.

**Live exercise practice focused on reasoning aloud**

Adequate evidence: A practised exercise where constraints were asked first — with a partner, or recorded solo on a supplied problem and labelled a rehearsal.

0 — Not practised.

1 — Practised, drawing first.

2 — Constraints asked, reasoning aloud.

3 — As adequate, and assumptions and checks were stated explicitly.

Repair: Practise again and do not draw until you have asked three questions. Recheck: The practice notes.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The last lesson turns all of this into a practice you can hold for months.

**Review criteria:**

- A fixed-time protocol with stated cuts exists
- Questions are prepared to ask before starting
- A decline is drafted for real product work
- Live exercise practice focused on reasoning aloud

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The appetite discipline applies directly. A take-home with a stated four hours means four hours; the correct response is to fix the time and cut the scope, then say exactly what you cut and why. Spending fourteen hours to look impressive misrepresents your speed and rewards a practice that harms everyone applying.
- Asking what is being assessed is legitimate and informative. If they are assessing research judgement, the deliverable is different from one assessing interface craft, and an employer who cannot answer has told you something useful about how the work is run.
- Declaring the time and the cuts is the strongest part of a submission. “Four hours: I did the structure, the main flow and the error states, and did not do visual polish or a second flow, because the states seemed more likely to be what you are assessing” demonstrates exactly the judgement the exercise is meant to test.
- Some take-homes are real work. A brief that asks you to redesign a live feature of their product, produce production-ready screens, or solve a problem their team is currently working on is asking for unpaid labour. Declining that, politely and with a reason, is the correct professional response, and an offer to do a shorter neutral exercise instead is reasonable.
- Live exercises assess reasoning, not output. Think aloud, ask about constraints and users, state your assumptions, and say what you would check. A confident wrong answer usually lands worse than a well-reasoned uncertain one. If nobody can set you a problem, take one of the supplied made-up problems, give yourself forty minutes and record yourself thinking aloud; label it a solo rehearsal.

[Ryan Singer: Shape Up](https://basecamp.com/shapeup/webbook).

</details>

## Lesson 12: Running the search as a practice

Stable ID: m20-l12-v1. Core.

A search runs for months, and the difference between a practice and a spiral is entirely in whether it is recorded and bounded.

Bring: The matrix, resume, profile and outreach records.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- A record table with what was sent and what happened
- A stated weekly cadence including rest
- A schedule for re-gathering vacancy evidence
- Written standing rules and a monthly review question set

### Start here: in everyday words

Career preparation turns your real work into clear evidence for a role, then practises explaining it without exaggeration. In this lesson, your first small result is: A record table with everything already sent entered into it.

**Words you will use**

- **The record:** One table. What was sent, when, by what route, and what came back. It is what makes the search improvable rather than merely long.
- **Backfilling:** Entering what you have already sent. It is usually more than you remember, and the response pattern starts there.
- **A held cadence:** A number you can sustain in month three, which is when it matters. Two applications and one message a week is a real pace.

**Quick example.** Made-up example. Setting a search cadence, and applying to everything. Eleven applications in the first week, every evening, anything that looked plausible. It felt like the only responsible way to treat something this important.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Your table: date, employer, role, the role’s eligibility, location and hours, route, what was sent, response and its date”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Review monthly against the matrix, not against your mood.

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Made-up example. The record is one table: date, employer, role, the role's eligibility, work location and hours, route, what was sent, response and date of response. Cadence: two applications and one outreach message a week, one rest day, reviewed on the first of each month. The vacancy pass is repeated every six weeks, taking about two hours the second time. The monthly review asks three questions: which matrix cells changed, which absence appears most often in refusals, and what the response pattern suggests about the messages rather than about her. Standing rules written and kept visible, including that nothing is automated and no unpaid production work is done.

**The record:** One table. What was sent, when, by what route, and what came back. It is what makes the search improvable rather than merely long.

**Backfilling:** Entering what you have already sent. It is usually more than you remember, and the response pattern starts there.

### Everything you have already sent, entered

### Set the cadence

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

A weekly cadence you could hold for three months, with a written rest day.

- Decide a weekly number you can hold for three months.
- Include a rest day and write it down.

**Start here:** Halve the number you first wrote, then ask whether you could hold it in month three.

**Enough:** The rest day is written down rather than intended.

**A held cadence:** A number you can sustain in month three, which is when it matters. Two applications and one message a week is a real pace.

**The written rest day:** Written because an unwritten one is the first thing a bad week takes, and a search with no rest days ends in month two.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Setting a search cadence, and applying to everything.

**What I did:** Eleven applications in the first week, every evening, anything that looked plausible. It felt like the only responsible way to treat something this important.

**What week three looked like:** Two applications, both rushed, and four evenings avoiding it entirely.

**What the record showed:** Of the eleven, nine were roles my matrix said I could evidence almost nothing for. The two considered ones both produced replies: two out of two, too few to prove anything, and enough to change how I spent my evenings.

**What volume had cost:** Not just the evenings. Nine hurried applications with a generic covering note, each carrying my name, sent to companies I might apply to properly later.

**What the cadence is now:** Two applications and one message a week, with a rest day written down. Slower in a week and far faster over three months, because it is still running.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is applying to everything to see what sticks, because volume feels like effort and effort feels like the responsible response to needing work. Volume without records teaches nothing and burns the effort you need in month three.

**Trade-off:** Two a week feels far too slow when you need work now, and it is the number that is still being met in week twelve.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether two is the right number for everybody. It is the number I could hold, which is the only test that matters.

**Enough:** Each question can be answered from the record or the matrix.

**From the record:** Which cells changed, which absence appears most often, what the response pattern says about the messages. All answerable from the table.

**About the messages, not about you:** The deliberate framing. A month of silence is evidence about routes and messages, and the record is what keeps it that way.

### Write the standing rules

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-brief.

Standing rules written while calm, kept where you will see them.

- Write the rules you will not break under pressure.
- Keep them where you will see them.

**Start here:** Write the rules as sentences beginning with I will not.

**Enough:** The rules are somewhere you will see them in a bad week.

**Written while calm:** Written now, because the pressure to break them arrives in month three when a rule you have not written is only a preference.

**The rules:** No fabricated experience, no inflated claims, no automation or bulk sending, no unpaid production work, one follow-up. Each one was earned somewhere in this course.

**Repair:** The one change a Check question asks you to make. Make it in the step it belongs to, then record here that you made it.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-demo.

Made-up example. Holding a standing rule in month three, and deciding case by case.

**Where I was:** Eleven weeks, thirty-one applications, two conversations and no offer. A role I wanted, whose listing asked for two years of professional experience.

**What I considered:** Describing the two years of self-directed work without the label. Not a lie exactly, and everything in the resume would still have been true.

**What made it stop:** A line I had written in week one saying every project carries its category label. Written calmly, when nothing was at stake, by somebody with no reason to be generous to himself.

**What I did instead:** Applied with the labels intact and a covering note naming the gap directly, saying what I had instead.

**What happened:** A refusal, politely. And in the same fortnight, a conversation with a company whose listing I would not have met either, which came from an outreach message rather than an application.

**Why the rule was the useful part:** Not because it produced the conversation. Because in month three I no longer had the judgement to make that decision well, and the rule made it for me.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is deciding case by case under pressure, because each individual case has a reasonable-sounding argument. Judgement is worst exactly when the stakes are highest, which is what the rules are for.

**Trade-off:** A rule holds you to a decision made without knowing the situation, and sometimes that costs you a real chance.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether the unlabelled version would have got an interview. It might have, and it would have been the first thing to fall apart in one.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: After six weeks looking for part-time translation work, Sam has sent forty applications, most in the first week. He keeps no record and says “nobody wants translators any more”. He remembers that his only two replies both came from agencies he had written to personally.

**Task:** Decide what he should set up for the next month and how he should review it. Explain why, including what his two replies can and cannot tell him.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Tells him to send more applications, or accepts “nobody wants translators” as the finding.
- Adequate: A simple record (date, organisation, route, what was sent, response), a weekly number he can hold with a rest day, and a monthly review from the record; the market claim is replaced by his own counts.
- Strong: Adequate, plus treats two replies from personal messages as a pattern worth testing, not proof, and dates any conclusion so it is re-checked rather than believed.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Running the search as a practice
What I am trying to do: Set up the ongoing method — records, cadence, re-gathering evidence and reviewing what you learn — and write the standing rules you will hold to.

Key idea or terms:
The record: One table. What was sent, when, by what route, and what came back. It is what makes the search improvable rather than merely long.
Backfilling: Entering what you have already sent. It is usually more than you remember, and the response pattern starts there.
A held cadence: A number you can sustain in month three, which is when it matters. Two applications and one message a week is a real pace.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Setting a search cadence, and applying to everything. Eleven applications in the first week, every evening, anything that looked plausible. It felt like the only responsible way to treat something this important.

Activity: Run a short interview rehearsal using only the supplied role and evidence. Ask one question at a time, then tell me where my answer needs a clearer example, decision or honest limit. Do not write the final answer for me.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Your table: date, employer, role, the role’s eligibility, location and hours, route, what was sent, response and its date” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Build the table and enter everything sent so far.
- Halve the number until it is one you could hold for three months.

- R48: [GitLab job families: product design management](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-families/product/product-design-management/) — How expectations, scope and progression are described over time. Purpose: Gives one published example of what growth is expected to look like after being hired. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. One employer's management ladder and its own measures of design success; it is not a career path, a standard, or evidence about roles in India. Fallback: R23.
- R37: [NN/g: confidence intervals and margins of error](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/confidence-interval/) — How sample size limits what a pattern can support. Purpose: Keeps the monthly review from drawing conclusions from a handful of responses. Free article, no account; the site sells courses and reports that are not required. Verified 2026-09-06. Written for usability metrics; applied here as a caution against reading a pattern into six replies. Fallback: R45.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**A record table exists and is populated**

Adequate evidence: A table with dates, routes, what was sent and responses.

0 — No record.

1 — Partial or retrospective.

2 — Complete and current.

3 — As adequate, and it already shows a pattern worth acting on.

Repair: Build the table and enter everything sent so far. Recheck: The record.

**A sustainable cadence including rest is stated**

Adequate evidence: A weekly number and a rest day, written down.

0 — No cadence, or an unsustainable one.

1 — A number without rest.

2 — A number you could hold for three months, with rest.

3 — As adequate, and exceeding it is treated as a warning.

Repair: Halve the number until it is one you could hold for three months. Recheck: The cadence.

**Vacancy evidence has a re-gathering schedule**

Adequate evidence: A stated interval and a note on dating old conclusions.

0 — Treated as permanent.

1 — Acknowledged as ageing.

2 — A scheduled re-gather with dated conclusions.

3 — As adequate, and the second pass is planned to be quicker.

Repair: Set an interval and mark every existing conclusion with its date. Recheck: The schedule.

**Standing rules and review questions are written**

Adequate evidence: Rules against fabrication, inflation, automation and unpaid production work, plus three review questions.

0 — Neither written.

1 — One of the two.

2 — Both written and kept visible.

3 — As adequate, and the review questions reference the matrix rather than feelings.

Repair: Write the rules you will not break and the questions you will ask monthly. Recheck: The rules and questions.

</details>

Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. This is the end of the course and the start of the practice: a portfolio, a matrix, a method for gathering your own evidence, and rules you wrote while you were calm.

**Review criteria:**

- A record table exists and is populated
- A sustainable cadence including rest is stated
- Vacancy evidence has a re-gathering schedule
- Standing rules and review questions are written

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The record is a small table and it does more than it looks. What was sent, to whom, on what date, and what came back — that is enough to see which of your messages get answered, which listings never respond, and whether the pattern is changing. Without it, months of effort become an undifferentiated feeling.
- Cadence should be sustainable. Two carefully written applications a week, held for three months, produces more than fifteen in one week followed by nothing. Decide the number, put it in the record, and treat exceeding it as a warning rather than as progress.
- The vacancy evidence expires. Your captures were current in one window and mostly will not exist in a month, so a search running past that needs a fresh pass, which is quicker the second time. Re-capture the three fit fields too, because an employer's eligibility, location or hours can change between postings. Anything you concluded from the old sample carries its date whenever you use it.
- Monthly review against the matrix is what keeps the search connected to reality: which cells changed, which absences still block, and whether replies changed after the gap you closed. With a handful of applications, a change in replies is a reason to look closer, not proof that the gap caused it. The alternative is reviewing against how you feel, which after eight weeks of silence is not an instrument you should trust.
- Write the standing rules now, while nothing is at stake: no fabricated experience, no inflated role, no automated outreach, no unpaid production work, no claim you cannot evidence, and a rest day. Under pressure these decisions get made badly, and a rule written in advance is what you fall back on.

[GitLab job families: product design management](https://handbook.gitlab.com/job-families/product/product-design-management/).

</details>
